Gibbs measures for contact Anosov flows are all exponentially mixing


Abstract
In this work we study strong spectral properties of Ruelle transfer operators related to Gibbs measures for contact Anosov flows. As a consequence we establish exponential decay of correlations for Hölder observables with respect to any Gibbs measure. The approach invented in 1997 by Dolgopyat, and further developed in our papers in 2011 and 2023, is substantially enhanced here, allowing to deal with the general case of arbitrary contact Anosov flows and arbitrary Gibbs measures. The results obtained here naturally apply to geodesic flows on compact Riemannian manifolds. As is now well-known, the strong spectral estimates for Ruelle operators and a well-established technique by Dolgopyat lead to exponential decay of correlations for Hölder continuous potentials. Other immediate consequences are: (a) existence of a non-zero analytic continuation of the Ruelle zeta function with a pole at the entropy in a vertical strip containing the entropy in its interior; (b) a Prime Orbit Theorem with an exponentially small error.



Luchezar Stoyanov

1
Introduction

More than 20 years ago Liverani
[
L
]
proved exponential decay of correlations for
C^{4}
contact Anosov flows
for the Sinai-Bowen-Ruelle measure determined by the Riemann volume.
In this work, as a consequence of the main result, we derive exponential decay of correlations
for
C^{5}
contact Anosov flows on compact Riemannian manifolds
M
with respect to
any Gibbs measure on
M
. In
[
St5
]
this was done for Gibbs measures admitting a Pesin set with exponentially
small tails
1
1

1

I.e. a Pesin set whose pre-images along the flow have measures decaying exponentially fast.
.
Here we succeed to establish exponential decay without any additional assumptions. As a consequence, for geodesic flows
on compact Riemannian manifolds every Gibbs measure is exponentially mixing.

As is now well-known, the study of statistical properties of continuous dynamical systems proved to be significantly more
difficult than the one for discrete systems. In particular the study of rates of correlation decay for Hölder continuous potentials
turned out to be highly non-trivial. After the extensive work of Sinai, Bowen and Ruelle in the 70’s on statistical properties of Anosov
diffeomorphisms, and the discovery in the 80’s by Ruelle
[
R
]
and Pollicott
[
Po
]
that for Axiom A flows on basic sets the decay
of correlations for Hölder potentials could be arbitrarily slow, there was a period of more than 15 years when it appeared no major results
in this area had been established. Significant breakthrough was achieved at the end of the 90’s.
First, it was Chernov
[
Ch1
]
who proved sub-exponential decay of correlations for Anosov flows on 3D
Riemannian manifolds (with respect
to the Sinai-Bowen-Ruelle measure). Then Dolgopyat
[
D1
]
proved exponential decay of correlations for Hölder continuous
potentials in two major cases: (i) geodesic flows on compact surfaces of negative curvature (with respect to any
Gibbs measure); (ii) transitive Anosov flows on compact Riemannian manifolds with
C^{1}
jointly non-integrable local
stable and unstable foliations (with respect to the Sinai-Bowen-Ruelle measure). The results in
[
D1
]
on decay of correlations were
derived as a consequence of some very strong spectral estimates for Ruelle transfer operators defined by means of a Markov family for the
flow. Dolgopyat’s paper
[
D1
]
nowadays is regarded as fundamental, not just for the results that it established
but also for the general framework
created there. The latter, or parts of it, has been used by various people to establish some very significant results: see e.g.
[
AGY
]
,

[
BaV
]
,
[
PoS
]
,
[
St1
]
,
[
N
]
,
[
OWi
]
,
[
PeS1
]
,
[
PeS2
]
,
[
DMS
]
, just to name a few of these.
Although Liverani studied a different Ruelle operator in
[
L
]
, at some stage he used, as he said, ”Dolgopyat’s cancellation mechanism”.
In our work
[
St3
]
we developed a modification of Dolgopyat’s approach to establish strong spectral properties for Ruelle transfer
operators for Axiom A flows on basic sets satisfying certain regularity conditions, and as a consequence established exponential decay of
correlations for arbitrary Gibbs measures for such flows. A much more sophisticated modification of
Dolgopyat’s framework was done in
[
St5
]
,
where we proved strong spectral properties for Ruelle transfer operators for
C^{3}
contact Anosov flows with respect to any Gibbs measure
admitting a Pesin set with exponentially small tails, and used this to establish exponential decay of correlations for such measures.
Recently Tsujii and Zhang
[
TZ
]
proved exponential decay of correlations for
arbitrary mixing measures for transitive Anosov flows on 3D compact manifolds, also using a modification of Dolgopyat’s appoach.

Another significant phase in the study of statistical properties of continuous dynamical systems originated from the works of Young

[
Y2
]
,
[
Y3
]
where she developed her so called ”tower method”. This was also a major event that prompted and facilitated
significant research activities and it turned out to be very useful in the study of both uniformly and non-uniformly hyperbolic systems –
see e.g.
[
Mel
]
and the references there. Various other approaches in studying decay of correlations
have been developed as well – see e.g.
[
D2
]
,
[
D3
]
,
[
MelV
]
and the references there.
Recently very sophisticated tools from PDE’s involving microlocal analysis have been
used in studying various properties of hyperboilic flows – decay of correlations, dynamical zeta functions,
distribution of Ruelle-Pollicott resonances –
see e.g.
[
NZ
]
,
[
DyG
]
,
[
DyZ
]
,
[
FaSj
]
,
[
FaT
]
and the references there,
just to mention a few of the large number of publications in
this area. And speaking about decay of correlations for hyperbolic systems we have to mention here the major result in
[
BaDL
]

about exponential decay of correlations for 2D Sinai billiards.

Let
\phi_{t}:M\longrightarrow M
be a
C^{2}
contact Anosov flow on a
C^{2}
compact Riemannian manifold
M
.
Let
\varphi=\phi_{1}
be the time-one map of the flow, and let
{\sf m}
be an
\varphi
-invariant probability measure on
M
.
Given
\alpha>0
denote by
C^{\alpha}(M)

the space of all
\alpha
-Hölder
complex-valued functions
on
M
, i.e. functions
h:M\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
for which there exists

L\geq 0
with
|h(x)-h(y)|\leq L\,(d(x,y))^{\alpha}
for all
x,y\in M
. For such
h
, let
|h|_{\alpha}
be the smallest possible
choice for
L
. Set
\|h\|_{0}=\sup_{x\in M}|h(x)|
, and
\|h\|_{\alpha}=\|h\|_{0}+|h|_{\alpha}
.

The main result in this paper is the following.

Theorem 1.1.

Let
\phi_{t}:M\longrightarrow M
be a
C^{5}
contact Anosov flow,
let
F_{0}
be a Hölder continuous function on
M
and let
{\sf m}
be the Gibbs measure
determined by
F_{0}
on
M
.
Then for every
\alpha>0
there exist constants
C=C(\alpha)>0
and
c=c(\alpha)>0
such that

\left|\int_{M}A(x)B(\phi_{t}(x))d{\sf m}(x)-\left(\int_{M}A(x)d{\sf m}(x)\right)\left(\int_{M}B(x)d{\sf m}(x)\right)\right|\leq Ce^{-ct}\|A\|_{\alpha}\|B\|_{\alpha}\;

for any two functions
A,B\in C^{\alpha}(M)
.

We obtain this as a consequence of Theorem 1.2 below and the procedure described in
[
D1
]
.
The assumption that the flow is
C^{5}
is made so that one can apply the procedure in
[
D1
]
.
In particular this is essential when estimating the Laplace transform of the correlation function

\rho(t)=\int_{M}A(x)B(\phi_{t}(x))\;d{\sf m}(x)-\left(\int_{M}A(x)\;d{\sf m}(x)\right)\left(\int_{M}B(x)\;d{\sf m}(x)\right)

(see part VI in Sect. 4 in
[
D1
]
).

To our knowledge there are several results known so far on
exponential decay of correlations for general Gibbs potentials: that
of Dolgopyat
[
D1
]
for geodesic flows on compact surfaces, the one in
[
St2
]
for Axiom A flows on basic sets
(under some additional assumptions); the recent result of Tsujii and Zhang for Anosov flows on 3D manifolds, and
the one in
[
St5
]
for contact Anosov flows on arbitrary compact manifolds but only for Gibbs measures admitting a Pesin
set with exponentially small tails.

Let
{\mathcal{R}}=\{R_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k_{0}}
be a (pseudo-) Markov partition for
\phi_{t}
consisting of
rectangles
R_{i}=[U_{i},S_{i}]
, where
U_{i}
(resp.
S_{i}
) are (admissible) subsets of
W^{u}_{\epsilon}(z_{i})

(resp.
W^{s}_{\epsilon}(z_{i})
) for some
\epsilon>0
and
z_{i}\in M
(cf. Sect. 2 for details).
The first return time function

\tau:R=\cup_{i=1}^{k_{0}}R_{i}\longrightarrow[0,\infty)

is essentially
\alpha_{1}
-Hölder continuous on
R
for some
\alpha_{1}>0
,
i.e. there exists a constant
L>0
such that if
x,y\in R_{i}\cap{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}(R_{j})
for some
i,j
,
where
{\mathcal{P}}:R\longrightarrow R
is the
standard Poincaré map, then
|\tau(x)-\tau(y)|\leq L\,(d(x,y))^{\alpha_{1}}
.
The
shift map

\sigma:U=\cup_{i=1}^{k_{0}}U_{i}\longrightarrow U

is defined by
\sigma=\pi^{(U)}\circ{\mathcal{P}}
, where

\pi^{(U)}:R\longrightarrow U
is the projection along the leaves of local stable manifolds.
Let
\widehat{U}
be the set of all
x\in U
whose orbits do not have common points with the boundary of
R
.
Given
\theta\in(0,1)
, as in
[
St5
]
, define the
metric
D_{\theta}
on
\widehat{U}

by
D_{\theta}(x,y)=0
if
x=y
,
D_{\theta}(x,y)=1
if
x,y
belong to different
U_{i}
’s and
D_{\theta}(x,y)=\theta^{N}
if

{\mathcal{P}}^{j}(x)
and
{\mathcal{P}}^{j}(y)
belong to the same rectangle
R_{i_{j}}
for all
j=0,1,\ldots,N-1
, and

N
is the largest integer with this property.

Denote by
{\mathcal{F}}_{\theta}(\widehat{U})

the space of all bounded functions
h:\widehat{U}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}

with Lipschitz constants

|h|_{\theta}=\sup\left\{\frac{|h(x)-h(y)|}{D_{\theta}(x,y)}:x\neq y;\ ;x,y\in\widehat{U}\right\}<\infty.

Apart from the standard norm
\|h\|_{\theta}=\|h\|_{0}+|h|_{\theta}
on
{\mathcal{F}}_{\theta}(\widehat{U})
, where
\|h\|_{0}=\sup_{x\in\widehat{U}}|h(x)|
,
as in
[
D1
]
, we will also use the special norm
\|.\|_{\theta,b}
defined by

\displaystyle\|h\|_{\theta,b}=\|h\|_{0}+\frac{|h|_{\theta}}{|b|}.

Consider a real-valued function
f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\theta_{0}}(\widehat{U})
for some small
\theta_{0}>0
. Then
f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\theta}(\widehat{U})

for all
\theta\in[\theta_{0},1)
. Set
g=g_{f}=f-P_{f}\tau
, where
P_{f}\in{\mathbb{R}}

is the unique number such that the topological pressure
\mbox{\rm Pr}_{\sigma}(g)
of
g
with respect to
\sigma
is zero (cf.
[
PP
]
).

We say that
Ruelle transfer operators related to
f
are eventually contracting

on
{\mathcal{F}}_{\theta}(\widehat{U})
if there exist constants
0<\rho<1
,
a_{0}>0
,

b_{0}\geq 1
,
T\geq 1
and
C>0
such that if
a,b\in{\mathbb{R}}
satisfy
|a|\leq a_{0}
and
|b|\geq b_{0}
, then

\|L_{f-(P_{f}+a+{\bf i}b)\tau}^{m}h\|_{\theta,b}\leq C\;\rho^{m}\;\|h\|_{\theta,b}

for any integer
m\geq T\log|b|
and any
h\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\theta}(\widehat{U})
.

This condition implies that the spectral radius of
L_{f-(P_{f}+a+{\bf i}b)\tau}
on

{\mathcal{F}}_{\theta}(\widehat{U})
does not exceed
\rho
. It is also easy to see that it implies the following
2
2

2

Which is the way we defined
eventual contraction of Ruelle transfer operators in
[
St2
]
, and it agrees with the way
the main result in
[
D1
]
is stated.
: for every
\epsilon>0
there exist constants
0<\rho<1
,
a_{0}>0
,

b_{0}\geq 1
and
C>0
such that if
a,b\in{\mathbb{R}}
satisfy
|a|\leq a_{0}
and
|b|\geq b_{0}
, then

\|L_{f-(P_{f}+a+{\bf i}b)\tau}^{m}h\|_{\theta,b}\leq C\;\rho^{m}\;|b|^{\epsilon}\;\|h\|_{\theta,b}

for any integer
m\geq 0
and any
h\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\theta}(\widehat{U})
.

The central result in this paper is the following.

Theorem 1.2.

Let
\phi_{t}:M\longrightarrow M
be a
C^{2}
contact Anosov flow
on a
C^{2}
compact Riemannian manifold
M
, let
{\mathcal{R}}=\{R_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k_{0}}
be a (pseudo-) Markov
partition for
\phi_{t}
as above and let
\sigma:U\longrightarrow U
be the corresponding shift map.
There exists constants
0<\theta_{1}<\theta<1
such that for any real-valued function
f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\theta_{1}}(\widehat{U})

the Ruelle transfer operators related to
f
are eventually contracting on
{\mathcal{F}}_{\theta}(\widehat{U})
.

Remark.
The constant
\theta
is defined by using some regularity properties of the flow
– see Sect. 6.1 below for details.
A slightly more general result can be established. Namely, one can prove the same result as above for every

f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\theta}
. This can be done repeating the lengthy approximation procedure in Sect. 8 in
[
St5
]
.
However the main consequences -- Corollary 1.3 below, as well as Theorems 1.1 and 1.3 -- will be
the same
3
3

3

Except for a better constant
\alpha_{0}>0
in Corollary 1.3.
.

A similar result for Hölder continuous functions (with respect to the Riemannian metric) looks a
bit more complicated, since in general Ruelle transfer operators do not preserve any of the spaces

C^{\alpha}(\widehat{U})
. However, they preserve a certain ‘filtration’
\cup_{0<\alpha\leq\alpha_{0}}C^{\alpha}(\widehat{U})
.
For
b\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
b\neq 0
, define the norm
\|.\|_{\alpha,b}
on
C^{\alpha}(\widehat{U})
by
\|h\|_{\alpha,b}=\|h\|_{0}+\frac{|h|_{\alpha}}{|b|}
.

Corollary 1.3.

Under the assumptions of Theorem 1.2, there exists a constant
\alpha_{0}\in(0,1]

such that for any real-valued function
f\in C^{\alpha_{0}}(\widehat{U})
the Ruelle transfer operators related to
f
are
eventually contracting on
\cup_{0<\alpha\leq\alpha_{0}}C^{\alpha}(\widehat{U})
. More precisely, there
exists a constant
\hat{\beta}\in(0,1]

and for each
\epsilon>0
there exist constants
0<\rho<1
,
a_{0}>0
,
b_{0}\geq 1
,

C>0
and
M>0
such that if
a,b\in{\mathbb{R}}

satisfy
|a|\leq a_{0}
and
|b|\geq b_{0}
, then for every integer
m\geq M\,\log|b|
and every

\alpha\in(0,\alpha_{0}]
the operator

\displaystyle L_{f-(P_{f}+a+{\bf i}b)\tau}^{m}:C^{\alpha}(\widehat{U})\longrightarrow C^{\alpha\hat{\beta}}(\widehat{U})

is well-defined and

\|L_{f-(P_{f}+a+{\bf i}b)\tau}^{m}h\|_{\alpha\hat{\beta},b}\leq C\;\rho^{m}\;\|h\|_{\alpha,b}

for every
h\in C^{\alpha}(\widehat{U})
.

The maximal constant
\alpha_{0}\in(0,1]
that one can choose above
is related to the regularity of the local stable/unstable foliations. Estimates for this constant
can be derived from certain bunching
condition concerning the rates of expansion/contraction of the flow along local unstable/stable
manifolds (see
[
Ha1
]
,
[
Ha2
]
,
[
PSW
]
).

The above was first proved by Dolgopyat (
[
D1
]
) in the case of geodesic flows on compact
surfaces of negative curvature with
\alpha_{0}=1
(then one can choose
\hat{\beta}=1
as well).
The second main result in
[
D1
]
concerns transitive Anosov flows on compact Riemannian
manifolds with
C^{1}
jointly non-integrable local stable and unstable foliations. For such
flows Dolgopyat proved that the conclusion of Corollary 1.3 with

\alpha_{0}=1
holds for the Sinai-Bowen-Ruelle potential
F_{0}=\log\det(d\phi_{\tau})_{|E^{u}}
.
More general results were proved in
[
St3
]
for mixing Axiom A flows on basic sets
(again for
\alpha_{0}=1
) under some additional regularity assumptions
4
4

4

As mentioned earlier,
the results apply e.g. to
C^{2}
mixing Axiom A flows on basic sets satisfying a certain pinching condition (similar to the

1/4
-pinching condition for geodesic flows on manifolds of negative curvature).
, and more recently
in
[
St5
]
for Gibbs measures for contact Anosov flows admitting a Pesin set with exponentially small tails.

Next, consider the
Ruelle zeta function

\zeta(s)=\prod_{\gamma}(1-e^{-s\ell(\gamma)})^{-1}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pts\in{\mathbb{C}},

where
\gamma
runs over the set of primitive closed orbits of
\phi_{t}:M\longrightarrow M

and
\ell(\gamma)
is the least period of
\gamma
. Denote by
h_{T}
the
topological entropy

of
\phi_{t}
on
M
.

Using Theorem 1.2 above and an argument of Pollicott and Sharp
[
PoS
]
, one derives the
following
5
5

5

As remarked in
[
St5
]
, instead of using the norm
\|\cdot\|_{1,b}
as in
[
PoS
]
, in the present
case one has to work with
\|\cdot\|_{\theta,b}
for some
\theta\in(0,1)
,
and then one has to use the so called Ruelle’s Lemma in the form proved in
[
W
]
.
This is enough to prove the estimate (2.3)
for
\zeta(s)
in
[
PoS
]
, and from there the arguments are the same.
.

Theorem 1.4.

Let
\phi_{t}:M\longrightarrow M
be a
C^{2}
contact Anosov flow on a
C^{2}
compact
Riemannian manifold
M
. Then:

(a)
The Ruele zeta function
\zeta(s)
of the flow
\phi_{t}:M\longrightarrow M
has an analytic and non-vanishing
continuation in a half-plane
\mbox{\rm Re}(s)>c
for some
c<h_{T}
except for a simple pole at
s=h_{T}
.

(b)
There exists
c\in(0,h_{T})
such that

\displaystyle\pi(\lambda)=\#\{\gamma:\ell(\gamma)\leq\lambda\}=\mbox{\rm li}(e^{h_{T}\lambda})+O(e^{c\lambda})

as
\lambda\to\infty
, where

\displaystyle\mbox{\rm li}(x)=\int_{2}^{x}\frac{du}{\log u}\sim\frac{x}{\log x}

as
x\to\infty
.

Parts (a) and (b) were first established by Pollicott and Sharp
[
PoS
]
for geodesic flows on
compact surfaces of negative curvature (using
[
D1
]
), and then similar results were proved in

[
St3
]
for mixing Axiom A flows on basic sets satisfying certain additional assumptions
(as mentioned above). More recently, using different methods, it was proved in
[
GLP
]
that:
(i) for volume preserving three dimensional Anosov flows (a) holds, and moreover, in the case of

C^{\infty}
flows, the Ruelle zeta function
\zeta(s)
is meromorphic in
{\mathbb{C}}
and
\zeta(s)\neq 0
for
\mbox{\rm Re}(s)>0
;
(ii) (b) holds for geodesic flows on
\frac{1}{9}
-pinched compact Riemannian manifolds of negative
curvature. These were obtained as consequences of more general results in
[
GLP
]
.

In Sect. 2 we collect some preliminary information used later on. Sect. 3 contains basic facts from Pesin’s theory
of Lyapunov exponents, some notation and three lemmas from
[
St2
]
and
[
St5
]
that we need in subsequent sections.
Sect. 4 deals with some technical results concerning cylinders defined by means of Markov families for general Anosov flows.
The most important fact in Sect. 4 is
Lemma 4.4 where for every unstable cylinder
{\mathcal{C}}
intersecting the given Pesin set
P_{0}
we construct two families of
sub-cylinders, each with a significant total measure
6
6

6

All considerations involve a given Gibbs measure
\nu
on
U
.
, and a certain
pairing between elements of the two families that is used later in Sects. 5 and 6 to develop a cancelations procedure for the so called
contraction operators
{\mathcal{N}}_{J}
. The construction in Lemma 4.4 is enhanced in Lemma 5.5, where we use significantly the contact form,
and establish a certain strong non-integrability property of the flow.

In Sect. 6 we define for a given parameter
b\in{\mathbb{R}}
, a family of cylinders
{\mathcal{C}}_{m}
with
lengths of size
\mbox{\rm Const}\;\log|b|
covering the given Pesin set
P_{0}
, and then use the constructions in Sects. 4 and 5 to define
nice families of sub-cylinders of these with appropriate pairings between their elements. The so called contraction operators are
then defined in a rather different way to what was done in previous papers. Here we use some large deviation arguments to get
some of the essential estimates.
As in
[
St5
]
, the contraction operators
{\mathcal{N}}_{J}
only contract in the vicinity of the Pesin set
P_{0}
– this is established in Sect. 7
using the families of sub-cylinders of the cylinders
{\mathcal{C}}_{m}
from Lemmas 4.4 and 5.5
and the particular pairing between these provided by the lemmas.

In Sect. 8 we succeed to obtain global contraction properties of the contraction operators.
Then using an appropriate modification of arguments from
[
D1
]
and
[
St5
]
we prove the main results in the paper.

2
Preliminaries

Let
M
be a
C^{2}
compact Riemannian manifold, and let
\phi_{t}:M\longrightarrow M

(
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
) be a
C^{2}
transitive
Anosov flow
on
M
.
This means that there exist constants
C>0
and
0<\lambda<1
such that there exists a
d\phi_{t}
-invariant decomposition

T_{x}M=E^{0}(x)\oplus E^{u}(x)\oplus E^{s}(x)
of
T_{x}M
(
x\in M
)
into a direct sum of non-zero linear subspaces, where
E^{0}(x)
is the one-dimensional subspace determined by the direction of the flow at
x
,

\|d\phi_{t}(u)\|\leq C\,\lambda^{t}\,\|u\|
for all
u\in E^{s}(x)
and
t\geq 0
, and

\|d\phi_{t}(u)\|\leq C\,\lambda^{-t}\,\|u\|
for all
u\in E^{u}(x)
and
t\leq 0
.
Throughout we denote by
\|\cdot\|
the
norm determined by the Riemannian metric on
M
.
Transitivity means that the flow has a dense orbit in
M
.

Given
x\in M
and a sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
the (strong)
stable
and
unstable manifolds

W_{\epsilon}^{s}(x)
and
W_{\epsilon}^{u}(x)
of size
\epsilon

are defined in the usual way. The corresponding tangent bundles are then

E^{u}(x)=T_{x}W_{\epsilon}^{u}(x)
and
E^{s}(x)=T_{x}W_{\epsilon}^{s}(x)
. Set

E^{u}(x;\delta)=\{u\in E^{u}(x):\|u\|\leq\delta\}
for any
\delta>0
and define

E^{s}(x;\delta)
similarly. Let

\exp^{u}_{x}:E^{u}(x;\epsilon)\longrightarrow W_{\epsilon}^{u}(x)
and
\exp^{s}_{x}:E^{s}(x;\epsilon)\longrightarrow W_{\epsilon}^{s}(x)

be the corresponding
exponential maps
.

The so called
temporal distance function

\Delta(x,y)
is defined as follows. Given a sufficiently small
\epsilon_{0}>0
,
it follows from the hyperbolicity of the flow on
M
that there exists
\epsilon_{1}\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
such that if
x,y\in M
and
d(x,y)<\epsilon_{1}
,
then there exists a unique point

[x,y]=W^{s}_{\epsilon}(x)\cap\phi_{[-\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{0}]}(W^{u}_{\epsilon_{0}}(y))

(cf.
[
KH
]
). Hence
\phi_{t}([x,y])\in W^{u}_{\epsilon_{0}}(y)
for some
t\in[-\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{0}]

and we set
7
7

7

A different definition for
\Delta
is given in
[
D1
]
and
[
L
]
, however
in the only case considered in this paper when
x\in W^{u}_{\epsilon}(z)
and

y\in W^{s}_{\epsilon}(z)
for some
z\in M
, these definitions coincide with the present one.

\Delta(x,y)=t
(
[
KB
]
,
[
D1
]
,
[
L
]
). For
x,y\in M
with
d(x,y)<\epsilon_{1}
, define
\pi_{y}(x)=[x,y]
.
In this way for every
y\in M
, on a small open neighbourhood
W
of
y
in
M
we get
a
projection

\pi_{y}:W\longrightarrow\phi_{[-\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{0}]}(W^{u}_{\epsilon_{0}}(y))
along local stable manifolds.
The map

\pi_{y}:\phi_{[-\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{1}]}(W^{u}_{\epsilon_{1}}(x))\longrightarrow\phi_{[-\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{0}]}(W^{u}_{\epsilon_{0}}(y))

is called a
local stable holonomy map
. In a similar way one defines
holonomy maps between any two sufficiently close local transversals to stable laminations (see e.g.
[
PSW
]
).
Combining such a map with a shift along the flow we get another local stable holonomy map

{\mathcal{H}}_{x}^{y}:W^{u}_{\epsilon_{1}}(x)\longrightarrow W^{u}_{\epsilon_{0}}(y).

In a similar way one defines local holonomy maps along unstable laminations.

For convenience of the reader we will now provide the definition of a Markov family for the flow (see e.g. Sect. 4 in
[
Ch2
]

for details).
Given a submanifold
D
of
M
of codimension one with
\mbox{\rm diam}(D)\leq\epsilon_{0}
which is transversal to the flow, the projection

\mbox{\rm pr}_{D}:\phi_{[-\epsilon,\epsilon]}(D)\longrightarrow D
along the flow is well-defined and smooth. For
x,y\in D
, set
\langle x,y\rangle_{D}=\mbox{\rm pr}_{D}([x,y])
.
A subset
\widetilde{R}
of
D
is called a
rectangle
if
\langle x,y\rangle_{D}\in\widetilde{R}
for all
x,y\in\widetilde{R}
. A
proper rectangle
is a rectangle
\widetilde{R}

that coincides with the closure of its interior in
D
. The stable and unstable leaves through
x\in\widetilde{R}
are defined by

W^{s}_{\widetilde{R}}(x)=\phi_{[-\epsilon,\epsilon]}(W^{s}_{\epsilon}(x))\cap\widetilde{R}
and
W^{u}_{\widetilde{R}}(x)=\phi_{[-\epsilon,\epsilon]}(W^{s}_{\epsilon}(x))\cap\widetilde{R}
.
By
\mbox{\rm Int}_{D}(A)
we denote the
interior
of a subset
A
of
D
in
D
.

Let
\widetilde{{\mathcal{R}}}=\{\widetilde{R}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k_{0}}
be a family of proper rectangles, where each
\widetilde{R}_{i}
is contained
in a submanifold
D_{i}
of
M
of codimension one and has the form

\widetilde{R}_{i}=\langle U_{i},S_{i}\rangle_{D_{i}}=\{\langle x,y\rangle_{D_{i}}:x\in U_{i},y\in S_{i}\}
,
where
U_{i}\subset W_{\epsilon}^{u}(z_{i})
and
S_{i}\subset W_{\epsilon}^{s}(z_{i})
, respectively,
for some
z_{i}\in M
.
Set

\displaystyle\widetilde{R}=\cup_{i=1}^{k_{0}}\widetilde{R}_{i}.

The family
\widetilde{{\mathcal{R}}}
is called
complete
if there exists a constant
\chi>0
such that for every
x\in M
,
\phi_{t}(x)\in\widetilde{R}
for some

t\in(0,\chi]
. The
Poincaré map

\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}:\widetilde{R}\longrightarrow\widetilde{R}

related to a complete family
\widetilde{{\mathcal{R}}}
is defined by
\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}(x)=\phi_{\tilde{\tau}(x)}(x)\in\widetilde{R}
, where

\tilde{\tau}(x)>0
is the smallest positive time with
\phi_{\tilde{\tau}(x)}(x)\in\widetilde{R}
. The function
\tilde{\tau}

is called the
first return time
associated with
\widetilde{{\mathcal{R}}}
.
A complete family
\widetilde{{\mathcal{R}}}=\{\widetilde{R}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k_{0}}
of rectangles in
M
is called a
Markov family

of size
\chi>0
for the flow
\phi_{t}
if:

(a)
\mbox{\rm diam}(\widetilde{R}_{i})<\chi
for all
i
;

(b) for any
i\neq j
and any
x\in\mbox{\rm Int}_{D}(\widetilde{R}_{i})\cap\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{-1}(\mbox{\rm Int}_{D}(\widetilde{R}_{j}))
we have

W_{\widetilde{R}_{i}}^{s}(x)\subset\overline{\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{-1}(W_{\widetilde{R}_{j}}^{s}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}(x)))}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\overline{\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}(W_{\widetilde{R}_{i}}^{u}(x))}\supset W_{\widetilde{R}_{j}}^{u}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}(x));

(c) for any
i\neq j
at least one of the sets
\widetilde{R}_{i}\cap\phi_{[0,\chi]}(\widetilde{R}_{j})
and
\widetilde{R}_{j}\cap\phi_{[0,\chi]}(\widetilde{R}_{i})
is empty.

The existence of a Markov family
\widetilde{{\mathcal{R}}}
of an arbitrarily small size
\chi>0
for
\phi_{t}
follows from the construction of Bowen
[
B1
]
.

As in
[
R
]
and
[
D1
]
, we can slightly change the Markov family
\widetilde{{\mathcal{R}}}
to a
pseudo-Markov family

{\mathcal{R}}=\{R_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k_{0}}
of
pseudo-rectangles

R_{i}=[U_{i},S_{i}]=\{[x,y]:x\in U_{i},y\in S_{i}\}
,
where
U_{i}
and
S_{i}
are as above. Set

\displaystyle R=\cup_{i=1}^{k_{0}}R_{i}.

Notice that
\mbox{\rm pr}_{D_{i}}(R_{i})=\widetilde{R}_{i}
for all
i
. For any
\xi=[x,y]\in R_{i}
set

W^{u}_{R_{i}}(\xi)=[U_{i},y]=\{[x^{\prime},y]:x^{\prime}\in U_{i}\}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptW^{s}_{R_{i}}(\xi)=[x,S_{i}]=\{[x,y^{\prime}]:y^{\prime}\in S_{i}\}\subset W^{s}_{\epsilon_{0}}(x).

The corresponding
Poincaré map

{\mathcal{P}}:R\longrightarrow R
is defined by

{\mathcal{P}}(x)=\phi_{\tau(x)}(x)\in R
, where
\tau(x)>0

is the smallest positive time with
\phi_{\tau(x)}(x)\in R
.
The
interior

\mbox{\rm Int}(R_{i})
of a rectangle
R_{i}
is defined by
\mbox{\rm pr}_{D}(\mbox{\rm Int}(R_{i}))=\mbox{\rm Int}_{D}(\widetilde{R}_{i})
. In a similar way one defines

\mbox{\rm Int}^{u}(A)
for a subset
A
of some
W^{u}_{R_{i}}(x)
and
\mbox{\rm Int}^{s}(A)
for a subset of
W^{s}_{R_{i}}(x)
.
The family
{\mathcal{R}}=\{R_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k_{0}}
has the same properties as
\widetilde{{\mathcal{R}}}
:

(a^{\prime})

\mbox{\rm diam}(R_{i})<\chi
for all
i
;

(b^{\prime})
for any
i\neq j
and any
x\in\mbox{\rm Int}(R_{i})\cap{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}(\mbox{\rm Int}(R_{j}))
we have

{\mathcal{P}}(\mbox{\rm Int}(W_{R_{i}}^{s}(x)))\subset\mbox{\rm Int}^{s}(W_{R_{j}}^{s}({\mathcal{P}}(x)))\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt{\mathcal{P}}(\mbox{\rm Int}(W_{R_{i}}^{u}(x)))\supset\mbox{\rm Int}(W_{R_{j}}^{u}({\mathcal{P}}(x)));

(c^{\prime})
for any
i\neq j
at least one of the sets
R_{i}\cap\phi_{[0,\chi]}(R_{j})
and
R_{j}\cap\phi_{[0,\chi]}(R_{i})
is empty.

Define the matrix
{\mathcal{A}}=({\mathcal{A}}_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{k_{0}}
by
{\mathcal{A}}_{ij}=1
if
{\mathcal{P}}(\mbox{\rm Int}(R_{i}))\cap\mbox{\rm Int}(R_{j})\neq\emptyset

and
{\mathcal{A}}_{ij}=0
otherwise. According to Sect. 2 in
[
BR
]
, we may assume that
{\mathcal{R}}

is chosen in such a way that
{\mathcal{A}}^{\tilde{p}_{0}}>0
(all entries of the
\tilde{p}_{0}
-fold product of
{\mathcal{A}}
by itself are
positive) for some integer
\tilde{p}_{0}>0
. In what follows we assume that the matrix
{\mathcal{A}}
has this property.

One should remark here that while in general
{\mathcal{P}}
and
\tau
are only (essentially) Hölder continuous,
the map
\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}
is (essentially) Lipschitz; see (2.1) below.

From now on we will assume that
\widetilde{{\mathcal{R}}}=\{\widetilde{R}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k_{0}}
is a
fixed Markov family
for the flow

\phi_{t}
of size
\chi<\epsilon_{1}/2<\epsilon_{0}/2<1
and that
{\mathcal{R}}=\{R_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k_{0}}
is the related pseudo-Markov family. Set

{\bf U=\cup_{i=1}^{k_{0}}U_{i}}

and
\displaystyle\mbox{\rm Int}^{u}(U)=\cup_{j=1}^{k_{0}}\mbox{\rm Int}^{u}(U_{j})
. It follows from the hyperbolicity of the flow that there exist
constants
d_{0}\in(0,1]
and
\gamma_{1}>\gamma>1
such that

d_{0}\gamma^{m}\;d(x,y)\leq d(\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{m}(x)),\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{m}(y))\leq\frac{\gamma_{1}^{m}}{d_{0}}d(x,y)

(2.1)

for all
x,y\in\widetilde{R}
such that
\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{j}(x),\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{j}(y)
belong to the same
\widetilde{R}_{i_{j}}
for all
j=0,1,\ldots,m
.
Fix constants
d_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}>\gamma>1
with these properties; these will be used throughout the whole paper.

Using the
projection

\pi^{(U)}:R\longrightarrow U
along stable leaves we define the

shift map

\sigma:U\longrightarrow U
by
\sigma=\pi^{(U)}\circ{\mathcal{P}}
.
We will use the same notation for the related projection
\pi^{(U)}:\widetilde{R}\longrightarrow U
along stable leaves which defines
another
shift map

\tilde{\sigma}:U\longrightarrow U
such that
\tilde{\sigma}=\pi^{(U)}\circ\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}
. The two shift maps

\sigma
and
\tilde{\sigma}
are naturally related. Notice that our definitions of
R
and
\widetilde{R}
are so that both contain
U

and both projections
\pi^{(U)}:R\longrightarrow U
and
\pi^{(U)}:\widetilde{R}\longrightarrow U
along stable leaves take values in
U
.

Notice that
\tau
is constant on each stable leaf
W_{R_{i}}^{s}(x)=W^{s}_{\epsilon_{0}}(x)\cap R_{i}
(however not
on the corresponding stable leaves
W_{\widetilde{R}_{i}}^{s}(x)=W^{s}_{\epsilon_{0}}(x)\cap\widetilde{R}_{i}
).
The shift map
\sigma
is naturally conjugate to the Bernoulli shift map
\sigma_{{\mathcal{A}}}:\Sigma_{{\mathcal{A}}}\longrightarrow\Sigma_{{\mathcal{A}}}

on the symbol space

\displaystyle\Sigma_{{\mathcal{A}}}=\{(i_{j})_{j=-\infty}^{\infty}:1\leq i_{j}\leq k_{0},{\mathcal{A}}_{i_{j}\;i_{j+1}}=1\>\>\mbox{ \rm for all }\>j\;\},

given by
\sigma_{{\mathcal{A}}}((i_{j}))=((i^{\prime}_{j}))
, where
i^{\prime}_{j}=i_{j+1}
for all
j
.
There exists a natural surjection
\pi:\Sigma_{{\mathcal{A}}}\longrightarrow\widetilde{R}

such that
\pi\circ\sigma_{A}={\mathcal{P}}\circ\pi
on a residual subset of
\widetilde{R}
(see e.g.
[
B1
]
or Sect. 4 in
[
Ch2
]
).
Denoting by
\widetilde{R}^{*}
be the set of those
x\in R
such that
\phi_{t}(x)\notin\mbox{\rm Int}(R_{j})
for any
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and any
j
,
we have
\pi\circ\sigma_{A}={\mathcal{P}}\circ\pi
on
\pi^{-1}(\widetilde{R}^{*})
.
Moreover
\pi
is Lipschitz on
\pi^{-1}(\widetilde{R}^{*})
if the latter is considered with the
metric

d_{\theta}
for some

\theta\in(0,1)
, defined by
d_{\theta}(\xi,\eta)=0
if
\xi=\eta
and
d_{\theta}(\xi,\eta)=\theta^{m}
if

\xi_{i}=\eta_{i}
for
|i|\leq m
and
m
is maximal with this property. Notice that

\hat{\tau}=\tau\circ\pi
defines a Lipschitz function on
\pi^{-1}(\widetilde{R}^{*})
,
so it has a Lipschitz extension
\hat{\tau}:\Sigma_{{\mathcal{A}}}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
(
[
B1
]
,
[
Ch2
]
).
The
space of Lipschitz functions
on
\Sigma_{{\mathcal{A}}}
with respect to the
metric
d_{\theta}
will be denoted by
C_{\theta}(\Sigma_{{\mathcal{A}}})
and will be considered with the norm

\|h\|_{\theta}=\|h\|_{0}+|h|_{\theta}
.

The
shift map

\sigma_{{\mathcal{A}}}:\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}\longrightarrow\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
on the one-sided subshift of finite type

\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}=\{(i_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}:1\leq i_{j}\leq k,{\mathcal{A}}_{i_{j}\;i_{j+1}}=1\>\>\mbox{ \rm for all }\>j\geq 0\;\},

is defined similarly.
Notice that
\hat{\tau}(\xi)=\tau(\pi(\xi))
depends only on the
forward coordinates of
\xi\in\Sigma_{{\mathcal{A}}}
.
In particular we can consider
\hat{\tau}
as a function on

\Sigma_{A}^{+}
such that
\hat{\tau}=\tau\circ\pi
on a residual subset of
\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
.
The metric
d_{\theta}
on
\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
and the space of Lipschitz functions
C_{\theta}(\Sigma_{{\mathcal{A}}}^{+})

are defined as for
\Sigma_{{\mathcal{A}}}
.
If
\hat{\pi}:\Sigma_{{\mathcal{A}}}\longrightarrow\Sigma_{{\mathcal{A}}}^{+}
is the
natural projection
, one shows easily that
there exists a continuous surjection
\pi^{+}:\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}\longrightarrow U
such that
then
\pi^{+}\circ\hat{\pi}=\pi^{(U)}\circ\pi
. Moreover,
\sigma\circ\pi^{+}=\pi^{+}\circ\sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
.

As stated in Sect. 1, we will denote by
\widehat{U}
the set of those
x\in U

such that
{\mathcal{P}}^{m}(x)\in\mbox{\rm Int}(R)=\cup_{i=1}^{k}\mbox{\rm Int}(R_{i})
for all
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. This is a residual subset
of
U
and has full measure with respect to any Gibbs measure on
U
(see e.g.
[
B1
]
).
Set
\widehat{U}_{i}=U_{i}\cap\widehat{U}
.

Let
B(U)
be the
space of bounded functions

g:U\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
with its standard norm
\|g\|_{0}=\sup_{x\in U}|g(x)|
.
Given a function
g\in B(\widehat{U})
, the
Ruelle transfer operator

L_{g}:B(U)\longrightarrow B(U)
is defined by

\displaystyle(L_{g}h)(u)=\sum_{\sigma(v)=u}e^{g(v)}h(v).

Via the natural projection
\pi^{+}:\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}\longrightarrow U
, the above corresponds to the well-known definition of a Ruelle transfer
operator on
\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
(see
[
Ba
]
,
[
B1
]
or
[
PP
]
).

Fix constants
0<\tilde{\tau}_{0}<\tau_{0}<\chi
so that

\tilde{\tau}_{0}\leq\tau(x)\leq\tau_{0}
for all
x\in R
.
We will assume that
\tilde{\tau}
satisfies the same estimates, namely
\tilde{\tau}_{0}\leq\tilde{\tau}(x)\leq\tau_{0}
for all
x\in\widetilde{R}
.
As we remarked earlier, in general
\tilde{\tau}
is not constant on the stable leaves
W_{\widetilde{R}_{i}}^{s}(x)=W^{s}_{\epsilon_{0}}(x)\cap\widetilde{R}_{i}
.
However, since
\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}
is Lipschitz, we can assume the constant
d_{0}\in(0,1]
is chosen so that

|\tilde{\tau}(\tilde{x})-\tilde{\tau}(\tilde{y})|\leq\frac{1}{d_{0}}d(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})

for all
\tilde{x},\tilde{y}\in\widetilde{R}_{i}
and all
i=1,\ldots,k
.

Throughout this paper
\alpha_{1}\in(0,1]
denotes a
fixed constant
such that
\tau\in C^{\alpha_{1}}(\widehat{U})
and the
local stable/unstable holonomy maps are uniformly
\alpha_{1}
-Hölder. Choosing the constant
d_{0}\in(0,1]
from (2.1)
sufficiently small, we will assume that for any
x,y\in R
the local stable holonomy map

{\mathcal{H}}_{x}^{y}:W^{u}_{\epsilon_{1}}(x)\longrightarrow W^{u}_{\epsilon_{0}}(y)

is so that

\displaystyle d({\mathcal{H}}_{x}^{y}(x^{\prime}),{\mathcal{H}}_{x}^{y}(x^{\prime\prime}))\leq\frac{1}{d_{0}}(d(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}))^{\alpha_{1}}

for all
x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}\in W^{u}_{\epsilon_{1}}(x)
.
We will also assume that a similar condition is satisfied by the corresponding local unstable holonomy maps.
Furthermore, we will assume that
\alpha_{1}
is chosen so that
the shift
\widetilde{\Psi}:R\longrightarrow\widetilde{R}
along the flow is
\alpha_{1}
-Hölder and satisfies a condition similar to the one about the
local holonomy maps.

3
Lyapunov exponents and Lyapunov regularity functions

Throughout this paper
M
denotes a
C^{2}
compact Riemannian manifold,
\phi_{t}
is a
C^{2}
Anosov flow
on
M
and
\varphi=\phi_{1}
. Let
F_{0}
be a Hölder continuous real-valued function on
M
and let

{\sf m}
be the Gibbs measure generated by
F_{0}
on
M
.

The
Oseledets Multiplicative Ergodic Theorem
(
[
Os
]
) implies
that in the situation considered here there exists a
\phi_{t}
-invariant subset
{\mathcal{L}}
of
M
with
{\sf m}({\mathcal{L}})=1
such that for every
x\in{\mathcal{L}}

there exist numbers

0<\chi_{1}<\chi_{2}<\ldots<\chi_{\tilde{k}}

and a
d\phi_{t}
-invariant decomposition

\displaystyle E^{u}(x)=E^{u}_{1}(x)\oplus E^{u}_{2}(x)\oplus\ldots\oplus E^{u}_{\tilde{k}}(x)

of
E^{u}(x)
into subspaces of constant dimensions such that

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\,\log\|d\phi_{t}(x)\cdot v\|=\chi_{i}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptv\in E^{u}_{i}(x)\setminus\{0\},

(3.1)

for all
i=1,\ldots,\tilde{k}
(see
[
BP
]
,
[
Ar
]
,
[
V
]
or
[
KH
]
).
Here and in what follows we denote by
\|\cdot\|
the
norm on the tangent spaces

T_{x}M
(
x\in M
) induced by the Riemannian metric
on
M
.

The numbers
\chi_{i}>0
are called (the positive)
Lyapunov exponents
of
\phi_{t}
.
In our case, the dimension
n_{i}
of
E^{u}_{i}(x)
is constant on
{\mathcal{L}}
, and clearly

n_{1}+n_{2}+\ldots+n_{\tilde{k}}=n^{u}=\dim(E^{u}(x))
for all
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
.
For
E^{s}(x)
,
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
, we have a similar decomposition involving the
corresponding negative Lyapunov exponents. For contact flows we have

n^{s}=\dim(E^{s}(x))=n^{u}
for all
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
, and the negative Lyapunov exponents are
-\chi_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,\tilde{k}
.

It follows from (3.1) that for every
\epsilon>0
, every
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
and every
i=1,\ldots,\tilde{k}
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\|d\varphi^{n}(x)_{|E^{u}_{i}(x)}\|}{e^{(\chi_{i}+\epsilon)n}}=0,

therefore

R_{0}(x)=\max_{1\leq i\leq\tilde{k}}\>\sup_{n\geq 0}\frac{\|d\varphi^{n}(x)_{|E^{u}_{i}(x)}\|}{e^{(\chi_{i}+\epsilon)n}}<\infty.

The function
R_{0}(x)
just defined is an example of a Lyapunov
\epsilon
-regularity function.

More generally, a Borel function
R_{\epsilon}:{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow(1,\infty)
such that

\frac{1}{R_{\epsilon}(x)}\leq\frac{\|d\varphi^{n}(x)\cdot v\|}{e^{(\chi_{i}+\epsilon)n}\,\|v\|}\leq R_{\epsilon}(x)\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptx\in{\mathcal{L}}\;,\;v\in E^{u}_{i}(x)\setminus\{0\}\;,\;n\geq 0,

(3.2)

for all
i=1,\ldots,\tilde{k}
, and

e^{-\epsilon}\leq\frac{R_{\epsilon}(\varphi(x))}{R_{\epsilon}(x)}\leq e^{\epsilon}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptx\in{\mathcal{L}},

(3.3)

is called a
Lyapunov
\epsilon
-regularity function
.
As in
[
PS
]
, by an
\epsilon
-slowly varying radius function

we mean a function of the form
r_{\epsilon}(x)=1/R_{\epsilon}(x)
,
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
, where
R_{\epsilon}
is a Lyapunov
\epsilon
-regularity function on
{\mathcal{L}}
.
For such
r_{\epsilon}
and
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
,
the linear map
d\varphi^{n}(x)
behaves on the ball
B^{u}(x,r_{\epsilon}(x))
as in the case of an uniformly hyperbolic flow –
see the relations (3.11) - (3.14) below.

In this paper by a
Pesin set
in
M
we mean a compact subset
P
of
{\mathcal{L}}
with
{\sf m}(P)>0
such that
there exist constants
\epsilon>0
and
C>0
and a Lyapunov

\epsilon
-regularity function
R_{\epsilon}(x)
on
{\mathcal{L}}
with
R_{\epsilon}(x)\leq C
for all
x\in P
. In a similar way we define Pesin sets in
R

(i.e. in
R\cap{\mathcal{L}}
) with respect to the induced measure
\mu
on
R
(see Sect. 4.2).
Clearly Pesin sets always exist.

In
[
St5
]
we considered Gibbs measures admitting

Pesin sets with exponentially small tails
, i.e. Pesin sets whose preimages along the flow have measures decaying exponentially fast.
Existence of such Pesin sets for a variety of Gibbs measures for hyperbolic diffeomorphisms and flows was established in
[
GSt
]
.
However, to our knowledge, there are no general results of this kind.

Set
\lambda_{i}=e^{\chi_{i}}
for all
i=1,\ldots,\tilde{k}
. Fix an arbitrary constant
\beta\in(0,1]
such that

\lambda_{j}^{1+\beta}<\lambda_{j+1}

(3.4)

for all
1\leq j<\tilde{k}.

Take
\hat{\epsilon}>0
so small that

\displaystyle e^{8\hat{\epsilon}}<\lambda_{1}
and
e^{8\hat{\epsilon}}<\lambda_{j}/\lambda_{j-1}
for all
j=2,\ldots,\tilde{k}
.
Some further assumptions about
\hat{\epsilon}
will be made later.
Set

1<\nu_{0}=\lambda_{1}e^{-8\hat{\epsilon}}<\mu_{j}=\lambda_{j}e^{-\hat{\epsilon}}<\lambda_{j}<\nu_{j}=\lambda_{j}e^{\hat{\epsilon}}

(3.5)

for all
j=1,\ldots,\tilde{k}
.

Fix
\hat{\epsilon}>0
with the above properties.

We will now provide some basic definitions and set-up from Sect. 3 in
[
St5
]
.
For
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
and
1\leq j\leq\tilde{k}
set

\widehat{E}^{u}_{j}(x)=E^{u}_{1}(x)\oplus\ldots\oplus E^{u}_{j-1}(x)\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\widetilde{E}^{u}_{j}=E^{u}_{j}(x)\oplus\ldots\oplus E^{u}_{\tilde{k}}(x).

Also set
\widehat{E}^{u}_{1}(x)=\{0\}
and
\widehat{E}^{u}_{\tilde{k}+1}(x)=E^{u}(x)
.
For any
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
and any
u\in E^{u}(x)
we will write
u=(u^{(1)},u^{(2)},\ldots,u^{(\tilde{k})})
, where
u^{(i)}\in E^{u}_{i}(x)
for all
i
.

According to results in the theory of non-uniformly hyperbolic systems (see
[
P
]
,
[
BP
]
)
for any
j=1,\ldots,\tilde{k}
the invariant bundle
\{\widetilde{E}^{u}_{j}(x)\}_{x\in{\mathcal{L}}}
is uniquely integrable over
{\mathcal{L}}
, i.e.
there exists a measurable
\varphi
-invariant family
\{\widetilde{W}^{u,j}_{\tilde{r}(x)}(x)\}_{x\in{\mathcal{L}}}

of
C^{2}
submanifolds
\widetilde{W}^{u,j}(x)=\widetilde{W}^{u,j}_{\tilde{r}(x)}(x)
of
M
tangent to the bundle
\widetilde{E}^{u}_{j}
for some

\hat{\epsilon}
-slowly varying radius function

\tilde{r}=\tilde{r}_{\hat{\epsilon}}:{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow(0,1)
.
Moreover, with
\beta\in(0,1]
as above, it follows from Theorem 6.6 in

[
PS
]
and (3.4) that there exists a
\varphi
-invariant family
\{\widehat{W}^{u,j}_{\tilde{r}(x)}(x)\}_{x\in{\mathcal{L}}}
of

C^{1+\beta}
submanifolds
\widehat{W}^{u,j}(x)=\widehat{W}^{u,j}_{\tilde{r}(x)}(x)

of
M
tangent to the bundle
\widehat{E}^{u}_{j}
for every
j>1
.
(However this family is not unique in general.) Fix
an
\varphi
-invariant family
\{\widehat{W}^{u,j}_{\tilde{r}(x)}(x)\}_{x\in{\mathcal{L}}}
with the latter properties for all
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
and
j=2,\ldots,\tilde{k}
.
Then there exist an
\hat{\epsilon}
-slowly varying radius function

r=r_{\hat{\epsilon}}:{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow(0,1)

and for any
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
a
C^{1+\beta}
diffeomorphism

\Phi_{x}^{u}:E^{u}(x;r(x))\longrightarrow\Phi_{x}(E^{u}(x;r(x))\subset W^{u}_{\tilde{r}(x)}(x)

such that

\Phi^{u}_{x}(\widehat{E}^{u}_{j}(x;r(x)))\subset\widehat{W}^{u,j}_{\tilde{r}(x)}(x)\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\Phi^{u}_{x}(\widetilde{E}^{u}_{j}(x;r(x)))\subset\widetilde{W}^{u,j}_{\tilde{r}(x)}(x)

(3.6)

for all
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
and
j=2,\ldots,\tilde{k}
. Moreover, since the submanifolds

\widehat{W}^{u,j}_{r(x)}(x)
and
\exp^{u}_{x}(\widehat{E}^{u}_{j}(x;r(x)))
of
W^{u}_{\tilde{r}(x)}(x)
are tangent at
x
of order

1+\beta
for each
j>1
, we can choose
\Phi^{u}_{x}
so that the diffeomorphism

\Psi^{u}_{x}=(\exp^{u}_{x})^{-1}\circ\Phi^{u}_{x}:E^{u}(x:r(x))\longrightarrow\Psi^{u}_{x}(E^{u}(x:r(x)))\subset E^{u}(x;\tilde{r}(x))

is
C^{1+\beta}
-close to identity. So, we can choose a Lyapunov
\hat{\epsilon}
-regularity function
R(x)=R_{\hat{\epsilon}}(x)
such that

\|\Psi^{u}_{x}(u)-u\|\leq R(x)\|u\|^{1+\beta}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\|(\Psi^{u}_{x})^{-1}(u)-u\|\leq R(x)\|u\|^{1+\beta}

(3.7)

for all
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
and
u\in E^{u}(x;r(x))
, and also that

\|d\Phi^{u}_{x}(u)\|\leq R(x)\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\|(d\Phi^{u}_{x}(u))^{-1}\|\leq R(x)\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptx\in{\mathcal{L}}\>,\>u\in E^{u}(x;r(x)).

(3.8)

More precisely, as in
[
LY2
]
(see (v) in (8.1) there), we will assume that there exists a global constant
R_{0}>0

such that for all
u,v\in E^{u}(x;r(x))
we have

\frac{1}{R_{0}}d(\Phi^{u}_{x}(u),\Phi^{u}_{x}(v))\leq\|u-v\|\leq R(x)\,d(\Phi^{u}_{x}(u),\Phi^{u}_{x}(v)).

(3.9)

For any
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
consider the
C^{1+\beta}
map

\displaystyle\hat{\varphi}_{x}=(\Phi^{u}_{\varphi(x)})^{-1}\circ\varphi\circ\Phi^{u}_{x}:E^{u}(x)\longrightarrow E^{u}(\varphi(x))

(defined locally near
0
).
We then have the relations

\hat{\varphi}^{-1}_{x}(\widehat{E}^{u}_{j}(\varphi(x);r(\varphi(x)))\subset\widehat{E}^{u}_{j}(x;r(x))\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\hat{\varphi}^{-1}_{x}(\widetilde{E}^{u}_{j}(\varphi(x);r(\varphi(x)))\subset\widetilde{E}^{u}_{j}(x;r(x))\;

for all
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
and
j>1
.

Given
y\in{\mathcal{L}}
and any integer
j\geq 1
we will use the notation

\hat{\varphi}_{y}^{j}=\hat{\varphi}_{\varphi^{j-1}(y)}\circ\ldots\circ\hat{\varphi}_{\varphi(y)}\circ\hat{\varphi}_{y}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\hat{\varphi}_{y}^{-j}=(\hat{\varphi}_{\varphi^{-j}(y)})^{-1}\circ\ldots\circ(\hat{\varphi}_{\varphi^{-2}(y)})^{-1}\circ(\hat{\varphi}_{\varphi^{-1}(y)})^{-1}\;,

at any point where these sequences of maps are well-defined.

It follows from well known results (see e.g. the Appendix in
[
LY1
]
, Sect. 8 in
[
LY2
]
or Sect. 3 in
[
PS
]
) that there exist a
Lyapunov
\hat{\epsilon}
-regularity function
\Gamma=\Gamma_{\hat{\epsilon}}:{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow[1,\infty)
and an
\hat{\epsilon}
-slowly varying
radius function
r=r_{\hat{\epsilon}}:{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow(0,1)
(we will assume that it is the same as the one chosen above)
and for each
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
a norm
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{x}
on
T_{x}M
such that

\|v\|\leq\|v\|^{\prime}_{x}\leq\Gamma(x)\|v\|\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptx\in{\mathcal{L}}\>,\>v\in T_{x}M,

(3.10)

and for any
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
and any integer
m\geq 0
, assuming

\hat{\varphi}_{x}^{j}(u),\hat{\varphi}_{x}^{j}(v)\in E^{u}(\varphi^{j}(x),r(\varphi^{j}(x)))
are well-defined for all
j=1,\ldots,m
, the following hold:

\mu^{m}_{j}\,\|u-v\|^{\prime}_{x}\leq\|\hat{\varphi}_{x}^{m}(u)-\hat{\varphi}_{x}^{m}(v)\|^{\prime}_{\varphi^{m}(x)}\leq\nu^{m}_{\tilde{k}}\,\|u-v\|^{\prime}_{x}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptu,v\in\widetilde{E}^{u}_{j}(x;r(x)),

(3.11)

\mu^{m}_{1}\,\|u-v\|^{\prime}_{x}\leq\|\hat{\varphi}_{x}^{m}(u)-\hat{\varphi}_{x}^{m}(v)\|^{\prime}_{\varphi^{m}(x)}\leq\nu_{\tilde{k}}^{m}\,\|u-v\|^{\prime}_{x}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptu,v\in E^{u}(x;r(x)),

(3.12)

\mu_{1}^{m}\,\|v\|^{\prime}_{x}\leq\|d\hat{\varphi}_{x}^{m}(u)\cdot v\|^{\prime}_{\varphi^{m}(x)}\leq\nu_{\tilde{k}}^{m}\,\|v\|^{\prime}_{x}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptu\in E^{u}(x;r(x))\;,\;v\in E^{u}(x),

(3.13)

\mu_{j}^{m}\,\|v\|^{\prime}_{x}\leq\|d\hat{\varphi}_{x}^{m}(0)\cdot v\|^{\prime}_{\varphi^{m}(x)}\leq\nu_{j}^{m}\,\|v\|^{\prime}_{x}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptv\in E^{u}_{j}(x).

(3.14)

Another useful norm is given by

|u|=\max\{\|u^{(i)}\|:1\leq i\leq\tilde{k}\}
, which is easily related to
\|\cdot\|
. Clearly,

\|u\|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{k}}\|u^{(i)}\|\leq\tilde{k}\,|u|.

Taking the regularity function
\Gamma(x)
appropriately, we have
|u|\leq\Gamma(x)\|u\|
, so

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tilde{k}}\,\|u\|\leq|u|\leq\Gamma(x)\|u\|
for all
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
and
u\in E^{u}(x)
.

Taylor’s formula (see also Sect. 3 in
[
PS
]
) implies that there exists
a Lyapunov
\hat{\epsilon}
-regularity function
G=G_{\hat{\epsilon}}:{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow[1,\infty)
such that for any
i=\pm 1

and any
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
we have

\hskip 10.00002pt\|\hat{\varphi}_{x}^{i}(v)-\hat{\varphi}_{x}^{i}(u)-d\hat{\varphi}^{i}_{x}(u)\cdot(v-u)\|\leq G(x)\,\|v-u\|^{1+\beta}\>\>,\>u,v\in E^{u}(x;r(x)),

and

\|d\hat{\varphi}^{i}_{x}(u)-d\hat{\varphi}^{i}_{x}(0)\|\leq G(x)\,\|u\|^{\beta}
for all
u\in E^{u}(x;r(x))
.

Fix a global constant
\beta>0
with the above properties. We will assume that
\beta

is chosen so that it satisfies (3.4) as well.

Next, for convenience of the reader we state three lemmas from
[
St3
]
and
[
St5
]
which will be used in Sect. 5 below.

Lemma 3.1.
(Lemma 3.3 in
[
St3
]
)
There exist a Lyapunov
\hat{\epsilon}
-regularity function

L:{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow[1,\infty)
and an
\hat{\epsilon}
-slowly varying radius function

r:{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow(0,1)

such that for any
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
, any integer
p\geq 1
and any
v\in E^{u}(z,r(z))
with
\|\hat{\varphi}_{z}^{p}(v)\|\leq r(x)
, where
z=\varphi^{-p}(x)
, we have

\|w^{(1)}_{p}-v^{(1)}_{p}\|\leq L(x)|v_{p}|^{1+\beta},

where
v_{p}=\hat{\varphi}^{p}_{z}(v)\in E^{u}(x)
and
w_{p}=d\hat{\varphi}_{z}^{p}(0)\cdot v\in E^{u}(x)
. Moreover, if
|v_{p}|=\|v^{(1)}_{p}\|\neq 0
, then

1/2\leq\|w^{(1)}_{p}\|/\|v^{(1)}_{p}\|\leq 2
.

Remark.
Notice that if
v\in E^{u}_{1}(z,r(z))
in the above lemma, then

v_{p},w_{p}\in E^{u}_{1}(x)
, so
\|w_{p}-v_{p}\|\leq L(x)\,\|v_{p}\|^{1+\beta}
.

We will now state some consequences from Sect. 10 in
[
St5
]
that apply to every Anosov flow.
For any
v=v^{(1)}+v^{(2)}+\ldots+v^{(\tilde{k})}\in E^{u}(x)
with
v^{(j)}\in E^{u}_{j}(x)
, set

\check{v}^{(2)}=v^{(2)}+\ldots+v^{(\tilde{k})}\in\widetilde{E}_{2}^{u}(x).

Repeating the proof of Lemma 3.5 in
[
St3
]
(with the above slightly different choices of
\hat{\mu}_{1}
,
\hat{\nu}_{1}
,
\hat{\mu}_{2}

and
\nu_{2}
; see also Lemma 10.1 in
[
St5
]
) we get the following.

Lemma 3.2.

Choosing the constant
\hat{\epsilon}^{\prime}\in(0,\hat{\epsilon})
sufficiently small, there exists an
\hat{\epsilon}^{\prime}
-slowly
varying radius function
\hat{r}(x)\leq r(x)
on
{\mathcal{L}}
such that for any
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
and any

V=V^{(1)}+\check{V}^{(2)}\in E^{u}(x;\hat{r}(x))
,
setting
y=\varphi^{-1}(x)
and
U=\hat{\varphi}_{x}^{-1}(V)
, we have

\displaystyle\|\check{U}^{(2)}\|^{\prime}_{y}\leq\frac{\|\check{V}^{(2)}\|^{\prime}_{x}}{\mu_{2}}
and

\displaystyle\|U^{(1)}\|^{\prime}_{y}\geq\frac{\|V^{(1)}\|^{\prime}_{x}}{\nu_{1}}
.

Lemma 3.3.
(Lemma 10.7(b) in
[
St5
]
)

There exist a
\frac{5\hat{\epsilon}}{\beta}
-slowly varying radius functions
\hat{r}(x)\leq r(x)

and a Lyapunov
4\hat{\epsilon}
-regularity function
L(x)
,
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
, such that
for any
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
and any integer
p\geq 1
, setting
z=\varphi^{-p}(x)
, the map

F^{p}_{x}=d\hat{\varphi}^{p}_{z}(0)\circ(\hat{\varphi}^{p}_{x})^{-1}:E^{u}(x;\hat{r}(x))\longrightarrow E^{u}(x;\hat{r}(x))

satisfies

\left\|\left[(F^{p}_{x}(a))^{(1)}-(F^{p}_{x}(b))^{(1)}\right]-[a^{(1)}-b^{(1)}]\right\|\leq L(x)\,\left(\|a-b\|^{1+\beta}+\|b\|^{\beta}\cdot\|a-b\|\right)

for all
a,b\in E^{u}(x;\hat{r}(x))
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|a-b\|\leq\left\|d\hat{\varphi}^{p}_{z}(0)\cdot\left[(\hat{\varphi}^{p}_{x})^{-1}(a)-(\hat{\varphi}^{p}_{x})^{-1}(b)\right]\right\|\leq 2\|a-b\|

for all
a,b\in E^{u}_{1}(x;\hat{r}(x))
.

4
Estimates and constructions involving cylinders

4.1
Cylinders defined by the Markov family

Let again
M
be a
C^{2}
compact Riemannian manifold and let
\phi_{t}
be a
C^{2}
transitive Anosov flow on
M
.

Here we do not assume that the flow is contact.

Choose a small
\hat{\epsilon}>0
, as in Sect. 3, but we may need to make it smaller later.
Throughout we will again assume that

R(x)
,
\Gamma(x)
,
D(x)
and
L(x)
are Lyapunov
\hat{\epsilon}
-regularity functions, while
r(x)
is
an
\hat{\epsilon}
-slowly varying radius function so that it satisfies (3.6) – (3.14) and the conclusions of Lemma 3.1.
Replacing
r(x)
with the smaller regularity function
\hat{r}(x)
,
without loss of generality we will assume that the conclusions of Lemmas 3.2 and 3.3 hold with
\hat{r}(x)
replaced by
r(x)
.

In what follows we assume that
\widetilde{{\mathcal{R}}}=\{\widetilde{R}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k_{0}}
is a fixed Markov falimiy for

\phi_{t}
on
M
of size
\chi<\epsilon_{1}/2<\epsilon_{0}/2
and
{\mathcal{R}}=\{R_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k_{0}}
is the related pseudo-Markov
family as in Sect. 2. We will use the notation associated with these from Sect. 2, and
we will assume that for any
i=1,\ldots,k_{0}
the point
z_{i}
is chosen so that
z_{i}\in\mbox{\rm Int}^{u}(W^{u}_{R_{i}}(z_{i}))
.
For any
x\in R
, any
y\in\widetilde{R}
and
\delta>0
set

B^{u}(x,\delta)=\{y\in W^{u}_{R_{i}}(x):d(x,y)<\delta\}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\widetilde{B}^{u}(y,\delta)=\{z\in W^{u}_{\widetilde{R}_{i}}(z):d(z,y)<\delta\}.

In a similar way define
B^{s}(x,\delta)
. The
open ball
with centre
x
and radius
r>0
on
M
with respect to
the Riemannian metric will be denoted simply by
B(x,r)
.

Given an unstable leaf
W=W^{u}_{R_{i}}(z)
in some rectangle
R_{i}

and an admissible sequence

{\imath}=(i_{0},\ldots,i_{m})
of integers
i_{j}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{0}\}
, the set

{\mathcal{C}}_{W}[{\imath}]=\{x\in W:{\mathcal{P}}^{j}(x)\in R_{i_{j}}\>,\>j=0,1,\ldots,m\}

will be called a
cylinder of length

m
in
W
(or an
unstable cylinder
in
R
in general).
When
W=U_{i}
we will simply write
{\mathcal{C}}[{\imath}]
.
In a similar way one defines cylinders
\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}_{V}[{\imath}]
,
where
V=W^{u}_{\widetilde{R}_{i}}(z)
is an unstable leaf in some rectangle
\widetilde{R}_{i}
.

Let

\mbox{\rm pr}_{D}:\cup_{i=1}^{k_{0}}\phi_{[-\epsilon,\epsilon]}(D_{i})\longrightarrow\cup_{i=1}^{k_{0}}D_{i}

be the
projection along the flow
, i.e. for all
i=1,\ldots,k_{0}
and all

x\in\phi_{[-\epsilon,\epsilon]}(D_{i})
we have
\mbox{\rm pr}_{D}(x)=\mbox{\rm pr}_{D_{i}}(x)
(see Sect. 2).
The shift along the flow determines bi-Hölder continuous bijections

\mathcal{T}_{z}:W^{u}_{\widetilde{R}}(z)\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}_{z}(W^{u}_{\widetilde{R}}(z))\subset W^{u}_{\epsilon_{0}}(z)\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\widetilde{\Psi}:R\longrightarrow\widetilde{R},

where both
\mathcal{T}_{z}
and
\widetilde{\Psi}:W^{u}_{R}(z)\longrightarrow W^{u}_{\widetilde{R}}(z)
are bijection for all
z\in S_{i}
and all
i
.

Given a cylinder
{\mathcal{C}}={\mathcal{C}}_{W}[{\imath}]
in some
R_{i}
we will frequently use the notation

\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}_{W}[{\imath}]=\widetilde{\Psi}({\mathcal{C}})
. This is then a cylinder of the same length in
\widetilde{R}_{i}
.
Sometimes it will be more convenient to work with the projection of the cylinders on actual
unstable manifolds, and we will use the notation
\widehat{{\mathcal{C}}}=\mathcal{T}_{z}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}})

for any cylinder
\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}
in some

\widetilde{R}_{i}
and any
z\in W^{u}_{\widetilde{R}_{i}}
. Then
\widehat{{\mathcal{C}}}\subset W^{u}_{\epsilon}(z)
. The map

\mathcal{T}_{z}:\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}\longrightarrow\widehat{{\mathcal{C}}}
is uniformly Lipschitz.
Clearly there exist global constants
0<\tilde{c}_{1}<\tilde{c}_{2}
, independent of
{\mathcal{C}}
and
z
such that

\tilde{c}_{1}\,\mbox{\rm diam}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}})\leq\mbox{\rm diam}(\widehat{{\mathcal{C}}})\leq\tilde{c}_{2}\,\mbox{\rm diam}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}).

(4.1)

Although the rectangles

\widetilde{R}_{i}
could have complicated structure
8
8

8

They are not connected in general except in 3D, as Chernov points out in Sect. 9
in
[
Ch1
]
.
, and could be rather ”fragmented”, each of them contains a non-empty open subset
9
9

9

In fact
\widetilde{R}_{i}
is the closure
of such an open subset of
D_{i}
.
of the corresponding submanifold
D_{i}
.
It is rather easy to show that there exists a constant
r_{0}>0
such that for every
i=1,\ldots,k_{0}
and every

x\in\widetilde{R}_{i}
there exists
y\in W^{u}_{\widetilde{R}_{i}}(x)
such that
\mbox{\rm dist}(y,\partial\widetilde{R}_{i})>r_{0}
and

B^{u}(y,r_{0})\cap\widetilde{R}_{i}\subset W^{u}_{\widetilde{R}_{i}}(x)
.
From now on we will assume that the constant
r_{0}>0
is chosen so that it has the property just described.
A few more restrictions on
r_{0}
will be imposed later.

4.2
The Gibbs measure
\nu
, the Ruelle operators
L_{ab}
and the metric
D_{\theta}

Let
F_{0}:M\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
be a Hölder continuous function and let

{\sf m}
be the
Gibbs measure
determined by
F_{0}
on
M
defined on the set
{\mathcal{L}}
of Lyapunov
regular points (
[
Si
]
,
[
B2
]
,
[
Ch2
]
,
[
PP
]
).
It induces a
Gibbs measure

\mu
on
R
(with respect to the Poincaré map
{\mathcal{P}}
) for the function

\displaystyle F(x)=\int_{0}^{\tau(x)}F_{0}(\phi_{s}(x))\,ds\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptx\in{\mathbb{R}}.

The latter is Hölder and, using Sinai’s Lemma, it is cohomologous to a
Hölder function
f:R\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}

which is constant on stable leaves in rectangles

R_{i}
in
R
.
Setting
g=f-P_{f}\tau
, where
P_{f}\in{\mathbb{R}}
is chosen so that the topological pressure of
g
with respect to the Poincaré map

{\mathcal{P}}:R\longrightarrow R
is
0
, we get a function on
R
that
depends on forward coordinates only,
so it can be considered as a function on
U
, i.e. on
\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
.

From now on in this paper we will assume that
f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\theta_{0}}(\widehat{U})
is a fixed real-valued function
for some small constant
\theta_{0}>0

and
g=f-P_{f}\,\tau
, where
P_{f}\in{\mathbb{R}}
is such that
\mbox{\rm Pr}_{\sigma}(g)=0
.
Notice that
{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau_{0}}(\widehat{U})\subset{\mathcal{F}}_{\theta}(\widehat{U})
for
0<\theta_{0}\leq\theta<1
.
In what follows we will consider parameters
\theta\in[\theta_{0},1)
.
Set
F^{(a)}=f-(P_{f}+a)\tau
.
By Ruelle-Perron-Frobenius’ Theorem (see e.g. Theorem 2.2 in
[
PP
]
) for any real number
a

with
|a|
sufficiently small, as an operator on the space
C(U)
of continuous functions
h:U\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
with the sup-norm
(which we identify with
C(\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}})
with the sup-norm),
L_{F^{(a)}}
has a
largest eigenvalue

\lambda_{a}
and
there exists a (unique) regular probability measure
\hat{\nu}_{a}
on
U
with
L_{F^{(a)}}^{*}\hat{\nu}_{a}=\lambda_{a}\,\hat{\nu}_{a}
, i.e.

\int L_{F^{(a)}}H\,d\hat{\nu}_{a}=\lambda_{a}\,\int H\ d\hat{\nu}_{a}

for every
H\in C(U)
. The corresponding eigenfunctions belong to
{\mathcal{F}}_{\theta_{0}}(\widehat{U})
.
Fix a corresponding (positive) eigenfunction
h_{a}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\theta_{0}}(\widehat{U})
such that
\int h_{a}\,d\hat{\nu}_{a}=1
.
Then
d\nu=h_{0}\,d\hat{\nu}_{0}
defines a
\sigma
-invariant probability measure
\nu
on
U
, called the
Gibbs measure

determined by the function
F^{(0)}
.

This is the measure on
U
that we will use throughout this paper.

Since
\mbox{\rm Pr}_{\sigma}(f-P_{f}\tau)=0
, it follows from the main properties of pressure (cf. e.g. chapter 3 in
[
PP
]
)
that
|\mbox{\rm Pr}_{\sigma}(F^{(a)})|\leq\|\tau\|_{0}\,|a|
. Moreover, for small
|a|
the maximal eigenvalue

\lambda_{a}
and the eigenfunction
h_{a}
are Lipschitz in
a
, so
there exist constants
a_{0}>0
and
C>0
such that
|h_{a}-h_{0}|\leq C\,|a|
on
\widehat{U}
and

|\lambda_{a}-1|\leq C|a|
for
|a|\leq a_{0}
.

We will identify
\mu
with the measure on
\widetilde{R}
defined by
\mu(\widetilde{\Psi}(A))=\mu(A)
for every Borel subset
A
of
R
.
Apart from that we will use the measure
\nu
on subsets of
\widetilde{\Psi}(U)
simply by setting
\nu(\widetilde{\Psi}(A))=\nu(A)

for every measurable subset
A
of
U
. The same will apply to subsets of
W^{u}_{\widetilde{R}}(x)
for
x\in\widetilde{R}
, identifying these with
subsets of
U
using projections along stable leaves in
\widetilde{R}
.

By a
Pesin set in
R
we mean a compact subset
P_{0}
of
R\cap{\mathcal{L}}
with
\mu(P_{0})>0
such that
the Lyapunov
\hat{\epsilon}
-regularity function
R(x)
is bounded on
P_{0}
.

Fix an arbitrary Pesin set
P_{0}
in
R\cap{\mathcal{L}}
and set
K_{0}=\pi^{(U)}(P_{0})
.

Since the Lyapunov
\hat{\epsilon}
-regularity function
R(x)
is bounded on
P_{0}
, the functions

\Gamma(x)
,
D(x)
and
L(x)
are also bounded above by some constants on
P_{0}
.
Similarly, the
\hat{\epsilon}
-slowly varying radius function
r(x)

is bounded below by some constant on
P_{0}
. Thus, we may assume that

R(x)\leq R_{0}\>\>,\>\>r(x)\geq r_{0}\>\>,\>\>\Gamma(x)\leq\Gamma_{0}\>\>,\>\>L(x)\leq L_{0}\>\>,\>\>D(x)\leq D_{0}

for all
x\in P_{0}

for some positive constants
R_{0},\Gamma_{0},L_{0},D_{0}\geq 1
and
r_{0}>0
. We fix
r_{0}>0

so that
r_{0}\leq\frac{1}{R_{0}}
.
We will also use the Pesin set
\widetilde{P}_{0}=\widetilde{\Psi}(P_{0})
in
\widetilde{R}
, and we will assume that the
functions
R(x)
,
r(x)
, etc. satisfy the same bounds as above on
\widetilde{P}_{0}
.

For
a,b\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
|a|\leq a_{0}
and
|b|\geq 1
, as in
[
D1
]
, consider the function

f^{(a)}(u)=f(u)-(P_{f}+a)\tau(u)+\ln h_{a}(u)-\ln h_{a}(\sigma(u))-\ln\lambda_{a}

and the operators

L_{ab}=L_{f^{(a)}-{\bf i}\,b\tau}:C(U)\longrightarrow C(U)\>\>\>,\>\>\>{\mathcal{M}}_{a}=L_{f^{(a)}}:C(U)\longrightarrow C(U).

Then
{\mathcal{M}}_{a}\;1=1
.
It is easy to see that

\displaystyle|(L_{ab}^{m}h)(u)|\leq({\mathcal{M}}_{a}^{m}|h|)(u)

for all
u\in U
,

h\in C(U)
and
m\geq 0
. Moreover,
L_{f^{(0)}}^{*}\nu=\nu
, i.e.

\displaystyle\int L_{f^{(0)}}H\,d\nu=\int H\,d\nu\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptH\in C(U).

For any integer
m\geq 1
and any function
h:U\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
define
h_{m}:U\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
by

h_{m}(u)=h(u)+h(\sigma(u))+\ldots+h(\sigma^{m-1}(u)).

Since
g
has zero topological pressure with respect to the shift map
\sigma:U\longrightarrow U
,
there exist constants
0<c_{1}\leq c_{2}

such that for any cylinder
{\mathcal{C}}={\mathcal{C}}^{u}[i_{0},\ldots,i_{m}]
of length
m
in
U
we have

c_{1}\leq\frac{\nu({\mathcal{C}})}{e^{g_{m}(y)}}\leq c_{2}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pty\in{\mathcal{C}},

(4.2)

(see e.g.
[
PP
]
). Moreover, we may assume that
g_{0}=\max g<0
and there exist constants

0<\rho_{1}\leq\rho_{2}<1
such that for some constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
as above we have

c_{1}\,\rho_{1}^{m}\leq\nu({\mathcal{C}})\leq c_{2}\,\rho_{2}^{m},

(4.3)

for every cylinder
{\mathcal{C}}={\mathcal{C}}^{u}[i_{0},\ldots,i_{m}]
of length
m
in
U

(see e.g. Proposition 2 in
[
Po
]
or pp. 54-55 in
[
Ch2
]
).

As in
[
St5
]
, here we will make a substantial use of the
metric
D_{\theta}
on
U
defined in Sect. 1 above.
For a non-empty subset
A
of
U
(or some
W^{u}_{R}(x)
) let
\mbox{\rm diam}_{\theta}(A)
be the
diameter
of
A
with respect to
D_{\theta}
.

Let the constants
d_{0}>0
,
1<\gamma<\gamma_{1}
and
\alpha_{1}>0
be as in Sect. 2.
Choose the constants
\alpha_{2}\in(0,1)
and
\theta\in[\theta_{0},1)
so that

\frac{1}{\gamma^{\alpha_{1}\beta}}\leq\theta\leq\frac{1}{(\gamma_{1})^{\alpha_{2}}}<1,

where
\beta\in(0,1)
is as in (3.4).
In particular
\alpha_{2}\in(0,1)
satisfies

\displaystyle 0<\alpha_{2}\leq\frac{\alpha_{1}\beta\,\log\gamma}{\log\gamma_{1}}.

The following lemma is similar to Lemma 5.1 in
[
St5
]
and its proof is the same.

Lemma 4.1.

Let
\theta\in(0,1)
be as above
.

(a)
For any cylinder
{\mathcal{C}}
in
U
the characteristic function
\chi_{{\mathcal{C}}}
of

{\mathcal{C}}
on
U
is Lipschitz with respect to
D_{\theta}
and
\mbox{\rm Lip}_{\theta}(\chi_{\mathcal{C}})\leq 1/\mbox{\rm diam}_{\theta}({\mathcal{C}})
.

(b)
There exists a constant
C_{1}>0
such that if
x,y\in\widehat{U}_{i}
for some
i
, then

|\tau(x)-\tau(y)|\leq C_{1}\,D_{\theta}(x,y).

That is,
\tau\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\theta}(\widehat{U})
.

(c)
There exists a constant
C_{1}>0
such that for any
z\in R
, any cylinder

{\mathcal{C}}
in
W^{u}_{R}(z)
and any
x,y\in{\mathcal{C}}
we have

d(\widetilde{\Psi}(x),\widetilde{\Psi}(y))\leq C_{1}\,D_{\theta}(x,y)
and

D_{\theta}(x,y)\leq C_{1}(d(\widetilde{\Psi}(x),\widetilde{\Psi}(y)))^{\alpha_{2}}
.
Therefore, for
\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}=\widetilde{\Psi}({\mathcal{C}})
we have

\displaystyle\mbox{\rm diam}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}})\leq C_{1}\,\mbox{\rm diam}_{\theta}({\mathcal{C}})\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{\rm diam}_{\theta}({\mathcal{C}})\leq C_{1}(\mbox{\rm diam}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}))^{\alpha_{2}}.

Using the analytical dependence of
h_{a}
and
\lambda_{a}
on
a
and assuming that the
constant
a_{0}>0
is sufficiently small, there exists
T_{0}=T_{0}(a_{0})>0
such that

T_{0}\geq\max\{\,\|f^{(a)}\|_{0}\,,\,|f^{(a)}_{|\widehat{U}}|_{\theta}\,,|\tau_{|\widehat{U}}|_{\theta}\,\}\;

(4.4)

for all
|a|\leq a_{0}
. We will assume from now on that
a_{0}>0
and
T_{0}>0
have these properties.
Taking the constant
T_{0}>0
sufficiently large, we have
\|f^{(a)}-f^{(0)}\|_{0}\leq T_{0}\,|a|

on
\widehat{U}
for
|a|\leq a_{0}
.

As in
[
D1
]
we have the following Lasota-Yorke type inequality (see Lemma 5.2 and its proof in the Appendix in
[
St5
]
).

Lemma 4.2.

There exists a constant
A_{0}>0
, depending on
\|f\|_{\theta}
, such that for all
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
with
|a|\leq a_{0}

the following hold: If the functions
h
and
H
on
\widehat{U}
and the constant
B>0
are such that
H>0
on
\widehat{U}
and

|h(v)-h(v^{\prime})|\leq B\,H(v^{\prime})\,D_{\theta}(v,v^{\prime})

for any
i
and any
v,v^{\prime}\in\widehat{U}_{i}
,
then for any
b\in{\mathbb{R}}
with
|b|\geq 1
and any integer
m\geq 1
we have

|L_{ab}^{m}h(u)-L_{ab}^{m}h(u^{\prime})|\leq A_{0}\,\left[B\,\theta^{m}\,({\mathcal{M}}_{a}^{m}H)(u^{\prime})+|b|\,({\mathcal{M}}_{a}^{m}|h|)(u^{\prime})\right]\,D_{\theta}(u,u^{\prime})

whenever
u,u^{\prime}\in\widehat{U}_{i}
for some
i=1,\ldots,k_{0}
.

4.3
Technical lemmas on sizes of cylinders

We continue with the notation and assumptions in Sects. 4.1 and 4.2. In addition we will assume that
the Markov family
{\mathcal{R}}
is chosen so that
6\tau_{0}<\beta,

where
\tau_{0}
is the constant from Sect. 2 and
\beta>0
is the constant with (3.4), fixed in Sect. 3.

The following technical lemma will be used significantly later on.

Lemma 4.3.

There exist a global constant
C_{3}>0
and constants
0<\hat{\epsilon}_{2}<\hat{\epsilon}_{1}

with
\hat{\epsilon}_{i}\leq\mbox{\rm const }\hat{\epsilon}
that can be made arbitrarily small with
\hat{\epsilon}
, such that if
\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}
is a cylinder of length
m

in
\widetilde{R}
with
\mbox{\rm diam}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}})<r_{0}
and
z_{0}\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}\cap\widetilde{P}_{0}
, then:

(a)
There exists an integer
k
with

m\hat{\epsilon}_{2}\leq k\leq m\hat{\epsilon}_{1}

such that
\displaystyle\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{m-k}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}})\subset B^{u}(z^{\prime},r(z^{\prime}))
, where
z^{\prime}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{m-k}(z_{0})
.

(b)
For
p=[\tilde{\tau}_{m-k}(z_{0})]
with
k
as above, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\varphi}_{z_{0}}^{p}(\widehat{{\mathcal{C}}})\subset B^{u}(z_{p},r(z_{p}))
, where
z_{p}=\varphi^{p}(z_{0})
and
\widehat{{\mathcal{C}}}=\mathcal{T}_{z_{0}}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}})
.
Moreover,

\displaystyle\mbox{\rm diam}(\varphi^{p}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}))\leq\frac{3C_{3}}{\gamma^{\alpha_{1}k}}\leq r_{0}e^{-p\hat{\epsilon}}\leq r(z_{p})
,
where
\alpha_{1}>0
is the Hölder constant from Sect. 2.

(c)
For every unstable cylinder
{\mathcal{C}}
of length
m
in
\widetilde{R}
and
z_{0}\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}\cap\widetilde{P}_{0}
,
we have

\frac{e^{-m\hat{\epsilon}_{7}/\tilde{\tau}_{0}}}{C_{3}\lambda_{1}^{m\tau_{0}}}\leq\frac{e^{-q\hat{\epsilon}_{7}}}{C_{3}\lambda_{1}^{q}}\leq\mbox{\rm diam}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}})\leq\frac{C_{3}\,e^{q\hat{\epsilon}_{7}}}{\lambda_{1}^{q}}\leq\frac{C_{3}e^{m\tau_{0}\,\hat{\epsilon}_{7}}}{\lambda_{1}^{m\tilde{\tau}_{0}}},

(4.5)

where
q=[\tilde{\tau}_{m}(z_{0})]
and
\hat{\epsilon}_{7}
is a constant with
0<\hat{\epsilon}_{7}<\mbox{\rm const }\,\hat{\epsilon}
.
Moreover, there exist
\hat{x}_{0}\in\mathcal{T}_{z_{0}}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}})
and a constant
0<\hat{\epsilon}_{5}<\mbox{\rm const }\,\hat{\epsilon}
such that

u_{0}=(\Phi^{u}_{z_{0}})^{-1}(\hat{x}_{0})\in E^{u}(z_{0},\epsilon_{1})
and we have

\|u^{(1)}_{0}\|\geq\frac{e^{-q\hat{\epsilon}_{7}}}{C\lambda_{1}^{q}}

(4.6)

where
C=2R^{2}_{0}\Gamma_{0}/c>0
for some global constant
c>0
.

Proof of Lemma.
4.3. We use some bits from the proof of Lemma 4.2(a) in
[
St5
]
, however we need a lot
more precision and details.

Let
\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}
be a cylinder of length
m
in
\widetilde{{\mathcal{R}}}
.
Fix an arbitrary
z_{0}\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}\cap\widetilde{P}_{0}
.
Since
m
is the length of
\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}
,
\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{m}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}})
contains a whole unstable leaf of a proper rectangle
\widetilde{R}_{j_{0}}
.
Let
Z_{m}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{m}(z_{0})\in\widetilde{R}_{j_{0}}
. Set
Z_{j}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{j}(z_{0})
and
z_{j}=\varphi^{j}(z_{0})
for
j\geq 1
.
By the choice of the constant
r_{0}>0
(see Sect. 4.1) there exists
y_{0}\in W^{u}_{\widetilde{R}_{j_{0}}}(Z_{m})
such that

\mbox{\rm dist}(y_{0},\partial\widetilde{R}_{j_{0}})>r_{0}
and
B^{u}(y_{0},r_{0})\cap\widetilde{R}_{j_{0}}\subset W^{u}_{\widetilde{R}_{j_{0}}}(Z_{m})
.
In particular, for every point
b^{\prime}\in B^{u}(y_{0},r_{0})

there exists
b\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}
with
\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{m}(b)=b^{\prime}
. Also,
\mbox{\rm diam}(\widetilde{R}_{j_{0}})\leq\chi
, so
\widetilde{R}_{j_{0}}\subset B^{u}(y_{0},2\chi)

and
\widetilde{R}_{j_{0}}\subset B^{u}(Z_{m},2\chi)
. Thus,
\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{m}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}})\subset B^{u}(Z_{m},2\chi)
.

We will now choose
k
with
0<k\leq m
so that

\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{m-k}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}})\subset B^{u}(Z_{m-k},r(Z_{m-k})),

(4.7)

where
Z_{m-k}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{m-k}(z_{0})=\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{-k}(Z_{m}).

Since
r(\cdot)
is a Lyapunov
\hat{\epsilon}
-regularity function (see Sect. 3), we have

r(Z_{m-k})\geq r(z_{0})e^{-(m-k)\hat{\epsilon}}\geq r_{0}e^{-(m-k)\hat{\epsilon}}
.
For every integer
0\leq k\leq m
, by (2.1) we have

\mbox{\rm diam}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{-k}(B^{u}(Z_{m-k},2\chi)))\leq\frac{2\chi}{d_{0}\gamma^{k}}
. Thus, (4.7) will be satisfied if

\frac{2\chi}{d_{0}\gamma^{k}}\leq r_{0}\,e^{-(m-k)\hat{\epsilon}}.

(4.8)

Let
k\geq 0
be a number with (4.8).
Then

\displaystyle\gamma^{k}e^{-(m-k)\hat{\epsilon}}\geq\frac{2\chi}{r_{0}d_{0}}
,
so

e^{-m\hat{\epsilon}}(\gamma e^{\hat{\epsilon}})^{k}\geq\mbox{\rm const }
, i.e.
(\gamma e^{\hat{\epsilon}})^{k}\geq\mbox{\rm const }e^{m\hat{\epsilon}}
.
Setting
\hat{\gamma}=\gamma e^{\hat{\epsilon}}
, we get

k\log\hat{\gamma}\geq m\hat{\epsilon}+\mbox{\rm const }

for some positive global constant
const
.

It follows from all the above that

\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{-k}(B^{u}(y_{0},r_{0}))\subset\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{m-k}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}})\subset\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{-k}(B^{u}(Z_{m},2\chi))\subset B^{u}(Z_{m-k},2\chi/(d_{0}\gamma^{k}))\subset B^{u}(Z_{m-k},r(Z_{m-k})).

(4.9)

Set
T=\tilde{\tau}_{m-k}(z_{0})
and
p=[T]
, so that
p\leq T<p+1
.

It follows from (2.1) and the
\alpha_{1}
-Hölder continuity of weak unstable manifolds of the flow
\phi_{t}
that
for every
z\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}
we have

|\tilde{\tau}_{m-k}(z)-\tilde{\tau}_{m-k}(z_{0})|\leq\mbox{\rm Const}\;\,d(\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{m-k}(z),\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{m-k}(z_{0}))\leq\mbox{\rm Const}\;\left(\frac{\chi}{d_{0}\gamma^{k}}\right)^{\alpha_{1}}\leq\frac{C}{\gamma^{\alpha_{1}k}}

(4.10)

for some global constant
C>0
. We will show now that

d(\varphi^{p}(z),\varphi^{p}(z_{0}))\leq\frac{3C}{\gamma^{\alpha_{1}k}}

(4.11)

for all
z\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}
.

Proof of
(4.11): Given
z\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}
, there are two cases to consider for
t=\tilde{\tau}_{m-k}(z)
and
T=\tilde{\tau}_{m-k}(z_{0})
.

Case 1.

t<p
. Then
t<p\leq T
and by (4.10),
p-t\leq T-t\leq\frac{C}{\gamma^{\alpha_{1}k}}

and
T-p\leq\frac{C}{\gamma^{\alpha_{1}k}}
. Thus,

\displaystyle d(\varphi^{p}(z),\varphi^{p}(z_{0}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(\phi_{p}(z),\phi_{p}(z_{0}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d(\phi_{p}(z),\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{m-k}(z))+d(\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{m-k}(z),\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{m-k}(z_{0}))+d(\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{m-k}(z_{0}),\phi_{p}(z_{0}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|p-t|+\frac{C}{\gamma^{\alpha_{1}k}}+|T-p|\leq\frac{3C}{\gamma^{\alpha_{1}k}}.

Case 2.

p\leq t
. First, assume that
t\leq T
. Then, using (4.10),

\displaystyle d(\varphi^{p}(z),\varphi^{p}(z_{0}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(\phi_{p}(z),\phi_{p}(z_{0}))\leq d(\phi_{t}(z),\phi_{t}(z_{0}))\leq d(\phi_{t}(z),\phi_{T}(z_{0}))+d(\phi_{T}(z_{0}),\phi_{t}(z_{0}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{m-k}(z),\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{m-k}(z_{0}))+|T-t|\leq\frac{2C}{\gamma^{\alpha_{1}k}}.

The other case to consider is
t>T
. Then
t>T\geq p
, and as above we get

\displaystyle d(\varphi^{p}(z),\varphi^{p}(z_{0}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(\phi_{p}(z),\phi_{p}(z_{0}))\leq d(\phi_{T}(z),\phi_{T}(z_{0}))\leq d(\phi_{t}(z),\phi_{T}(z_{0}))+d(\phi_{t}(z),\phi_{T}(z))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{m-k}(z),\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{m-k}(z_{0}))+|T-t|\leq\frac{2C}{\gamma^{\alpha_{1}k}}.

This proves (4.11). It implies that

\mbox{\rm diam}(\varphi^{p}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}))\leq\frac{3C}{\gamma^{\alpha_{1}k}}.

(4.12)

For
z_{p}=\varphi^{p}(z_{0})
we have

r_{p}=r(z_{p})\geq r_{0}\,e^{-p\hat{\epsilon}}
. We need to have

r(z_{p})>\mbox{\rm diam}(\varphi^{p}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}))\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{\rm and }\hskip 10.00002ptr(z_{p})\geq\mbox{\rm diam}(\hat{\varphi}^{p}(\widehat{{\mathcal{C}}})).

(4.13)

By (4.12) and (4.1), for this it would be enough to have

\displaystyle 3C\tilde{c}_{2}\,e^{-\alpha_{1}k\,\log\gamma}\leq r_{0}e^{-p\hat{\epsilon}}
,
that is
-(\alpha_{1}\log\gamma)k\leq\log(r_{0}/(3C\tilde{c}_{2}))-p\hat{\epsilon}
which is equivalent to

\displaystyle p\hat{\epsilon}\leq\log\frac{r_{0}}{3C\tilde{c}_{2}}+(\alpha_{1}\log\gamma)k
.
Since
p=[\tilde{\tau}_{m-k}(z_{0})]\leq(m-k)\tau_{0}
, we have

p\hat{\epsilon}\leq(m-k)\tau_{0}\hat{\epsilon}
, and so the above holds if

(m-k)\tau_{0}\hat{\epsilon}\leq(\alpha_{1}\log\gamma)k-D_{0},

with
D_{0}=|\log\frac{r_{0}}{3C\tilde{c}_{2}}|
, that is if

m\tau_{0}\hat{\epsilon}\leq(\tau_{0}\hat{\epsilon}+\alpha_{1}\log\gamma)k-D_{0}.

(4.14)

We will now assume that

m\hat{\epsilon}_{2}\leq k\leq m\hat{\epsilon}_{1},

(4.15)

where
\hat{\epsilon}_{2}=\frac{\hat{\epsilon}\tau_{0}}{\alpha_{1}\log\gamma}
is a small number
(can be made arbitrarily small choosing the initial
\hat{\epsilon}
small), and (there is a lot freedom in this choice) e.g.

\hat{\epsilon}_{1}=2\hat{\epsilon}_{2}
. Then for
m\geq m_{0}
sufficiently large,
m\hat{\epsilon}_{2}\leq k
implies

m\tau_{0}\hat{\epsilon}\leq(\alpha_{1}\log\gamma)k<(\tau_{0}\hat{\epsilon}+\alpha_{1}\log\gamma)k-D_{0},

assuming
k>1
is sufficiently large,
so (4.14) holds and therefore (4.13) holds as well. The latter yields

\varphi^{p}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}})\subset B^{u}(z_{p},r(z_{p}))\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{\rm and}\hskip 10.00002pt\hat{\varphi}^{p}_{z_{0}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{C}}})\subset B^{u}(z_{p},r(z_{p})).

(4.16)

This completes the proofs of parts (a) and (b).

Proof of part
(c). We will continue to use the notation introduced above.

Since
p=[\tilde{\tau}_{m-k}(z_{0})]
, we have
\tilde{\tau}_{m-k}(z_{0})=p+t
for some
t\in[0,1)
.
For
y^{\prime}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{-k}(y_{0})
, by (2.1) and (4.9), it follows that

B^{u}(y^{\prime},d_{0}r_{0}/\gamma_{1}^{k})\subset\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{-k}(B^{u}(y_{0},r_{0}))\subset\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{m-k}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}})=\phi_{\tilde{\tau}_{m-k}(z_{0})}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}).

Setting
y^{\prime\prime}=\phi_{-t}(y^{\prime})
and
\hat{y}=\mathcal{T}_{z_{0}}(y^{\prime\prime})
and using (4.1), we have

B^{u}(y^{\prime\prime},d_{0}r_{0}/\gamma_{1}^{k+1})\subset\phi_{\tilde{\tau}_{m-k}(z_{0})-t}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}})=\varphi^{p}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}})\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{\rm and}\hskip 10.00002ptB^{u}(\hat{y},d_{0}r_{0}\tilde{c}_{1}/\gamma_{1}^{k+1})\subset\hat{\varphi}_{z_{0}}^{p}(\widehat{{\mathcal{C}}}).

By (4.15),

d_{0}r_{0}\tilde{c}_{1}/\gamma_{1}^{k+1}=\frac{d_{0}r_{0}\tilde{c}_{1}}{\gamma_{1}}e^{-k\log\gamma_{1}}\geq\frac{d_{0}r_{0}\tilde{c}_{1}}{\gamma_{1}}e^{-m\hat{\epsilon}_{1}\,\log\gamma_{1}}=c^{\prime}_{3}\,e^{-m\hat{\epsilon}_{3}},

for some global constant
c^{\prime}_{3}>0
and
\hat{\epsilon}_{3}=(\log\gamma_{1})\,\hat{\epsilon}_{1}>0
. Hence, taking into account (4.16) as well, we obtain

B^{u}(\hat{y},c^{\prime}_{3}\,e^{-m\hat{\epsilon}_{3}})\subset\hat{\varphi}_{z_{0}}^{p}(\widehat{{\mathcal{C}}})\subset B^{u}(z_{p},r(z_{p})).

(4.17)

and

B^{u}(y^{\prime\prime},c^{\prime\prime}_{3}\,e^{-m\hat{\epsilon}_{3}})\subset\varphi^{p}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}})\subset B^{u}(z_{p},r(z_{p}))

(4.18)

with
c^{\prime\prime}_{3}=c^{\prime}_{3}/\tilde{c}_{1}
.
Then for every
b^{\prime}\in B^{u}(\hat{y},c^{\prime}_{3}\,e^{-m\hat{\epsilon}_{3}})
there exists
b\in\widehat{{\mathcal{C}}}
with
\hat{\varphi}_{z_{0}}^{p}(b)=b^{\prime}
.
Notice also that
r=c^{\prime}_{3}e^{-m\hat{\epsilon}_{3}}\leq r(z_{p})
.

To apply the map
(\Phi^{u}_{z_{p}})^{-1}
to (4.17) we will use (3.9) and

R(z_{p})\leq R(z_{0})e^{\hat{\epsilon}\,p}\leq R_{0}e^{\hat{\epsilon}\,(m-k)\tau_{0}}\leq R_{0}e^{\hat{\epsilon}\,m\,\tau_{0}}
.
Setting
\xi=(\Phi^{u}_{z_{p}})^{-1}(\hat{y})
,
c_{3}=c^{\prime}_{3}/R_{0}
and
\hat{\epsilon}_{4}=\hat{\epsilon}_{3}+\hat{\epsilon}\tau_{0}
,
it follows from (4.17) that

(\Phi^{u}_{z_{p}})^{-1}(B^{u}(\hat{y},c_{3}\,e^{-m\hat{\epsilon}_{4}}))\subset B(\xi,R(z_{p})c_{3}\,e^{-m\hat{\epsilon}_{4}})\subset B(\xi,r)\subset E^{u}(z_{p}).

An elementary argument shows that in the normed
space
E^{u}(z_{p})
we can always find an element
\hat{\xi}
in the ball
B(\xi,r)
in
E^{u}(z_{p})
such that
\|\hat{\xi}^{(1)}\|\geq r/2
. Indeed, assume
e.g.
\xi^{(1)}\geq 0
in the natural coordinates in
E^{u}(z_{p})
. If
\xi^{(1)}\geq r/2
just take
\hat{\xi}=\xi
. If
0\leq\xi^{(1)}<r/2
, take

\hat{\xi}=(\xi^{(1)}+r/2,\xi^{(2)},\ldots,\xi^{(n_{u})})
, where
n_{u}=\dim(E^{u}(z_{p}))
.
We have

v_{0}=\Phi^{u}_{z_{p}}(\hat{\xi})\in\Phi^{u}_{z_{p}}(B(\xi,r))\subset B^{u}(z_{p},r(z_{p}))\cap\hat{\varphi}_{z_{0}}^{p}(\widehat{{\mathcal{C}}}).

Set
u_{0}=\hat{\varphi}^{-p}_{z_{p}}(v_{0})\in E^{u}(z_{0})
. For
v_{0}
the above gives
\|v^{(1)}_{0}\|_{z_{p}}\geq\|\hat{\xi}^{(1)}\|\geq r/2
, while

\hat{x}_{0}=\Phi^{u}_{z_{0}}(u_{0})\in\widehat{{\mathcal{C}}}
. Hence

\displaystyle\mbox{\rm diam}(\widehat{{\mathcal{C}}})\geq d(z_{0},\hat{x}_{0})\geq\frac{\|u_{0}\|}{R_{0}}\geq\frac{\|u^{(1)}_{0}\|^{\prime}_{z_{0}}}{\Gamma_{0}R_{0}}
, and
by (4.1),

\displaystyle\mbox{\rm diam}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}})\geq\frac{\tilde{c}_{1}}{\Gamma_{0}R_{0}}\,\|u^{(1)}_{0}\|^{\prime}_{z_{0}}.

Notice also that for
\hat{x}_{0}=\Phi^{u}_{z_{0}}(u_{0})

we have
\hat{\varphi}^{p}_{z_{0}}(u_{0})=v_{0}\in\hat{\varphi}_{z_{0}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{C}}})
.
Thus, for
x_{0}=(\mathcal{T}_{z_{0}})^{-1}(\hat{x}_{0})\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}
we have
\varphi^{p}(x_{0})\in\varphi^{p}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}})
.

Recall that
p=[\tilde{\tau}_{m-k}(z_{0})]
.
It follows from
q=[\tilde{\tau}_{m}(z_{0})]
that
m\,\tilde{\tau}_{0}-1\leq q\leq m\tau_{0}
, while (4.15) yields

q\geq p+[\tilde{\tau}_{k}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{P}}}^{p}(z_{0}))]\geq p+k\tilde{\tau}_{0}-1\geq p+m\tilde{\tau}_{0}\hat{\epsilon}_{2}-1\geq p+q\frac{\tilde{\tau}_{0}\hat{\epsilon}_{2}}{\tau_{0}}-1.

Similarly, using (4.15) again, we get
q\leq p+q\frac{\tau_{0}\hat{\epsilon}_{1}}{\tilde{\tau}_{0}}+\frac{\tau_{0}\hat{\epsilon}_{1}}{\tilde{\tau}_{0}}+1
,
therefore

q(1-\frac{\tau_{0}\hat{\epsilon}_{1}}{\tilde{\tau}_{0}})-2\leq p\leq q(1-\frac{\tilde{\tau}_{0}\hat{\epsilon}_{2}}{\tau_{0}})+1.

Thus, for sufficiently large
m
(then
q
is also large) we have

q(1-\hat{\epsilon}_{6})\leq p\leq q(1-\hat{\epsilon}_{5})\leq q,

(4.19)

for some small constants
0<\hat{\epsilon}_{5}\leq\mbox{\rm const }\hat{\epsilon}
and
0<\hat{\epsilon}_{6}\leq\mbox{\rm const }\hat{\epsilon}
.

Now
\nu_{1}=\lambda_{1}e^{\hat{\epsilon}}
and
p\leq q
give

\|u^{(1)}_{0}\|^{\prime}_{z_{0}}\geq\frac{\|v^{(1)}_{0}\|^{\prime}_{z_{p}}}{\nu_{1}^{p}}\geq\frac{r}{2\nu_{1}^{p}}\geq\frac{c^{\prime}_{3}\,e^{-m\hat{\epsilon}_{3}}}{2\nu_{1}^{q}}\geq\frac{c^{\prime}_{3}\,e^{-(q+1)\hat{\epsilon}_{3}/\tilde{\tau}_{0}}}{2\nu_{1}^{q}}\geq\frac{c^{\prime}_{3}\,e^{-(q+1)\hat{\epsilon}_{3}/\tilde{\tau}_{0}-q\hat{\epsilon}}}{2\lambda_{1}^{q}}\geq\frac{c^{\prime}_{3}\,e^{-q\hat{\epsilon}_{7}-\hat{\epsilon}_{3}/\tilde{\tau}_{0}}}{2\lambda_{1}^{q}},

(4.20)

for some
0<\hat{\epsilon}_{7}\leq\mbox{\rm const }\,\hat{\epsilon}
.
Hence
\mbox{\rm diam}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}})\geq\frac{e^{-q\hat{\epsilon}_{7}}}{C_{3}\lambda_{1}^{q}}
, taking
C_{3}\geq 2R_{0}\Gamma_{0}/(\tilde{c}_{1}c^{\prime}_{3}e^{1/\tilde{\tau}_{0}})>0
.

This proves the left-hand-side inequality in (
4.5
).

We will now prove in a similar way the other inequality in (
4.5
).

It follows from (4.18) and (4.16) and the choice of
k
and
p
that
