Appendix II

Proof of Lemma
6.2. Let
\epsilon\in(0,\theta/3)
. Then
\epsilon
is an expansivity constants for
\sigma_{A}:\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}\longrightarrow\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
.
As mentioned in Remark 1 in Sect. 6.2, given
\varphi\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\theta}(\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}})
there exists a constant
K_{\varphi}
so that

|\varphi_{n}(x)-\varphi_{n}(y)|\leq K_{\varphi}\>\>\mbox{\rm for all }\>\>x,y\in\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}\>\>\mbox{\rm with }\>\>D_{\theta}(\sigma^{i}(x),\sigma^{i}(y))<\epsilon\>\forall\>i=0,1,\ldots,n-1.

(10.1)

We can take e.g.
K_{\varphi}=\frac{|\varphi|_{\theta}}{1-\theta}
.

Since the matrix
{\mathcal{A}}
satisfies
{\mathcal{A}}^{\tilde{p}_{0}}>0
for some integer
\tilde{p}_{0}>0
(see Sect. 2), the shift has the

specification property
(see e.g. p. 60 in
[
Ba
]
): for every
\delta>0
there exists an integer
p=p(\delta)>0
so that
for all points
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\in\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
and all integers
n_{1},n_{2},\ldots n_{\ell}>0

and
p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{\ell}\geq p
there exists
z\in\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
such that

D_{\theta}(\sigma^{m(j-1)+i}(z),\sigma^{i}(x_{j}))\leq\delta
,
where
m_{j}=n_{1}+\ldots+n_{j}+p_{1}+\ldots+p_{j}
, for all
i=0,1,\ldots,n_{j}-1
and all
j=1,2,\ldots,\ell
.

We will use this property with
\delta=\epsilon
.
Fix
p=p(\epsilon)
with the property described in the above definition.

Given an integer
n\geq 1
and
\delta>0
, a subset
E
of
\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
is called

(n,\delta)
-separated
if for every
x,y\in E
,
x\neq y
, there exists
j=0,1\ldots,n-1
so that

D_{\theta}(\sigma^{j}(x),\sigma^{j}(y))>\delta
.
We say that
E
is a maximal
(n,\delta)
-separated set
if whenever
E\subset E^{\prime}
and
E^{\prime}
is an
(n,\delta)
-separated set, we have
E=E^{\prime}
.

For any
\delta\in(0,\theta)
,
x\in\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
n\geq 1
, the open ball

B_{n}(x,\delta)=\{y\in\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}:D_{\theta}(\sigma^{i}(x),\sigma^{i}(y))<\delta\;,\;i=0,1,\ldots,n-1\}

is contained in the cylinder
{\mathcal{C}}[x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1}]
in
\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
, so by (4.2),

\nu(B_{n}(x,\delta))\leq c_{2}e^{g_{n}(x)}
. Moreover, if
\theta^{r}\leq\delta
for some integer
r\geq 0
,
then
{\mathcal{C}}_{n+r}[x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n+r-1}]\subset B_{n}(x,\delta)
, so

\nu(B_{n}(x,\delta))\geq c_{1}e^{g_{n+r}(x)}\geq c_{1}(\delta)e^{g_{n}(x)}
. So we have something similar to (4.2):

c_{1}(\delta)e^{g_{n}(x)}\leq\nu(B_{n}(x,\delta))\leq c_{2}e^{g_{n}(x)},

where
c_{1}(\delta)\geq c_{1}e^{rg_{0}}
. Set
c_{1}^{\prime}=c_{1}(\epsilon)\leq c_{1}
.

For any finite
E\subset\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
,
n\geq 1
and
\varphi\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\theta}(\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}})
set

Z_{n}(\varphi,E)=\sum_{x\in E}e^{\varphi_{n}(x)}.

It is known (see e.g.
[
HR
]
and
[
B3
]
) that

\displaystyle\mbox{\rm Pr}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\log Z_{n}(\varphi,E_{n})

for any choice of the
(n,\epsilon)
-separated sets
E_{n}
in
\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
. Moreover, we know from Lemma 3 in
[
B3
]

how fast this convergence is ocuring. We will describe this now in details
16
16

16

Although Lemmas 1 - 3 in
[
B3
]

are proved for homeomorphisms
f:X\longrightarrow X
on compact metric spaces
X
, the arguments in the
proofs work for the shift
\sigma_{A}:\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}\longrightarrow\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
.
.

We state a bit more precise version of the first lemma which is what we need here for the one-sided shift

\sigma_{A}:\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}\longrightarrow\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
. We sketch the proof for completeness, although it is almost the same as
the one in
[
B3
]
.

Lemma 10.1.
(Lemma 1 in
[
B3
]
)
Let
\epsilon>0
be as above. There exists
an integer
N=N(\epsilon)\geq 1
with the following property: for every
\delta\in[\epsilon/2,2\epsilon]
there exists a constant

\displaystyle C_{\delta,\epsilon}=C_{\delta,\epsilon}(\varphi)\leq Me^{K_{\varphi}}

for some global constant
M=M(\epsilon)>0
depending on
\epsilon
only such that

Z_{n}(\varphi,\delta)\leq C_{\delta,\epsilon}\,Z_{n}(\varphi,\epsilon)

for all integers
n>N
.

Sketch of Proof.

Let
\epsilon/2\leq\delta\leq\epsilon
.
Then choose the minimal integer
N\geq 0
so that

D_{\theta}(\sigma^{k}(x),\sigma^{k}(y))\leq 2\epsilon
for all
k\leq N
implies
D_{\theta}(x,y)\leq\delta
.
It is enough to choose
N
so that
\theta^{N}\leq\delta
, i.e. we need
\theta^{N}\leq\epsilon/2
. Then one has to choose

\alpha>0
so that
D_{\theta}(x,y)\leq\alpha
implies
D_{\theta}(\sigma^{k}(x),\sigma^{k}(y))\leq\delta
for all
k\leq N
.
We can simply take
\alpha=\theta^{N}\delta
, i.e.
\alpha=\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{4}
. Let
M=M(\epsilon)
be the maximum number
of points in the product metric space
\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}\times\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
(with the maximum metric) so that any two points are at least a distance

\alpha=\epsilon^{2}/4
apart.

Let
n>N
. Consider now an arbitrary
(n,\delta)
-separated set
E
and a
maximal
(n,\epsilon)
-separated set
F
in
\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
. Then for any
x\in E
there exists
a(x)\in F
with

D_{\theta}(\sigma^{i}(x),\sigma^{i}(a(x)))\leq\epsilon
for all
i<n
.

Given any
a\in F
, set
E_{a}=\{x\in E:a(x)=a\}
. We will show that
|E_{a}|\leq M
.
It is enough to show that
X=\{(x,\sigma^{n-N}(x)):x\in E_{a}\}
has at most
M
distinct elements.
If
|X|>M
, then by the choice of
M
, there exist
x,y\in X
,
x\neq y
, with
either
D_{\theta}(x,y)\leq\alpha
(and then
D_{\theta}(\sigma^{i}(x),\sigma^{i}(y))\leq\delta
for all
0\leq i\leq N
),
or
D_{\theta}(\sigma^{n-N}(x),\sigma^{n-N}(y))\leq\alpha
(and then
D_{\theta}(\sigma^{n-N+i}(x),\sigma^{n-N+i}(y))\leq\delta

for all
0\leq i\leq N
).
Now
x,y\in E_{a}
gives

D_{\theta}(\sigma^{i}(x),\sigma^{i}(y))\leq D_{\theta}(\sigma^{i}(x),\sigma^{i}(a))+D_{\theta}(\sigma^{i}(a),\sigma^{i}(y))\leq 2\epsilon

for all
i<n
. Then the choice of
N
implies that
D_{\theta}(\sigma^{i+N}(x),\sigma^{i+N}(y))\leq\delta
.
This shows that
D_{\theta}(\sigma^{k}(x),\sigma^{k}(y))\leq\delta
for all
k<n
, which is a contradiction, since

E
is
(n,\delta)
-separated. Thus,
|X|\leq M
, so we have
|E_{a}|\leq M
for all
a\in F
.

Now using
|\varphi_{n}(x)-\varphi_{n}(a)|\leq K_{\varphi}
for
x\in E_{a}
, we derive

\sum_{x\in E}e^{\varphi_{n}(x)}\leq\sum_{a\in F}\sum_{x\in E_{a}}e^{\varphi_{n}(x)}\leq\sum_{a\in F}\sum_{x\in E_{a}}e^{K_{\varphi}}e^{\varphi_{n}(a)}\leq Me^{K_{\varphi}}Z_{n}(\varphi,\epsilon).

This proves the lemma.

Fix an integer
N=N(\epsilon)
with the property in Lemma 10.1.

Lemma 10.2.
(Lemma 2 in
[
B3
]
)
There exists constants
D_{\epsilon}=D_{\epsilon}(\varphi)>0

and
E_{\epsilon}=E_{\epsilon}(\varphi>0
such that

E_{\epsilon}\,\prod_{j=1}^{k}Z_{n_{j}}(\varphi,\epsilon)\leq Z_{n_{j}+n_{j+1}+\ldots+n_{k}}(\varphi,\epsilon)\leq D_{\epsilon}\,\prod_{j=1}^{k}Z_{n_{j}}(\varphi,\epsilon)

for all integers
n_{1},n_{2},\ldots,n_{k}\geq N
and all
k\geq 1
. Moreover we can take

D_{\epsilon}=D_{\epsilon}(\varphi)=e^{K_{\varphi}}\,C_{\frac{\epsilon}{2},\epsilon}(\varphi)

(10.2)

and

E_{\epsilon}=E_{\epsilon}(\varphi)=\frac{e^{-p\,\|\varphi\|_{0}+K_{\varphi}}}{D_{\epsilon}(\varphi)\,C_{\epsilon,3\epsilon}(\varphi)\,\max\{1,Z_{p}(\varphi,\epsilon)\}}=\frac{e^{-p\,\|\varphi\|_{0}}}{C_{\epsilon/2,\epsilon}\,C_{\epsilon,3\epsilon}(\varphi)\,\max\{1,Z_{p}(\varphi,\epsilon)\}}.

(10.3)

The proof of this lemma is given in all details in
[
B3
]
, and it works without change in our case using the above Lemma 10.1.
As a consequence of the above, in the same way as in
[
B3
]
, one obtains

Lemma 10.3.
(Lemma 3 in
[
B3
]
)
With
\epsilon>0
as above, we have

\frac{1}{D_{\epsilon}(\varphi)}\,e^{n\mbox{\rm Pr}_{\sigma}(\varphi)}\leq Z_{n}(\varphi,\epsilon)\leq\frac{1}{E_{\epsilon}(\varphi)}\,e^{n\mbox{\rm Pr}_{\sigma}(\varphi)}

(10.4)

for all integers
n\geq N
.

We now continue with the proof of Lemma 6.2. We follow the argument in the first half of the proof of Lemma 4.1 in
[
MV
]
.

Fix an arbitrary
q_{0}<0
and consider
q\in[q_{0},0]
. Let
|b|\geq b_{0}
and let the constant
K>0
satisfy the assumptions in Lemma 6.2.
We will use Lemmas 10.1, 10.2 and 10.3 with
\varphi=\varphi(q)=g+q\Psi
. Notice that for a sufficiently large global constant
C_{1}=C_{1}(q_{0})>0
,
the constant
K_{\varphi}=C_{1}K
satisfies (10.1) for
\varphi
.
Moreover, since
p
is a constant, we can choose
C_{1}
so that
\|p\varphi\|_{0}\leq C_{1}
and

\max\{1,Z_{p}(\varphi,\epsilon)\}\leq C_{1}

for any choice of
q\in[q_{0},0]
. Thus,
|E_{\epsilon}(\varphi)|\leq C_{2}(\epsilon)
for some global constant
C_{2}(\epsilon)
depending only on
\epsilon
.

Let
E_{n}=\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}\}
be a maximal
(n,\epsilon)
-separated set in
\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
. Then
\cup_{i=j}^{k}B_{n}(y_{j},\epsilon)=\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
.
Thus, for every
x\in\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
there exists
j\leq k
with

D_{\theta}(\sigma^{i}(x),\sigma^{i}(y_{j}))\leq\epsilon
for all
i=0,1,\ldots,n-1
. This implies
|\Psi_{n}(x)-\Psi_{n}(y_{j})|\leq K
.
Hence for any
q\in{\mathbb{R}}
we have

\displaystyle e^{nf_{n}(q)}=\int_{\Sigma^{+}_{{\mathcal{A}}}}e^{q\Psi_{n}(x)}\,d\nu(x)\leq\sum_{j=1}^{k}\int_{B_{n}(y_{j},\epsilon)}e^{q\Psi_{n}(x)}\,d\nu(x)\leq\sum_{j=1}^{k}e^{|q|K+q\Psi_{n}(y_{j})}\,\nu(B_{n}(y_{j},\epsilon)).

Since
\nu(B_{n}(y_{j},\epsilon))\leq c_{2}\,e^{g_{n}(y_{j})}
, it follows that

\displaystyle e^{nf_{n}(q)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c_{2}e^{|q|K}\sum_{j=1}^{k}e^{q\Psi_{n}(y_{j})+g_{n}(y_{j})}=c_{2}e^{|q|K}Z_{n}(g+q\Psi,E_{n}).

This and (10.4) with
\varphi=g+q\Psi
imply

\displaystyle f_{n}(q)\leq\frac{\log c_{2}+|q|K}{n}+\frac{1}{n}\log Z_{n}(g+q\psi,E_{n})\leq\mbox{\rm Pr}_{\sigma}(g+q\Psi)+\frac{\log c_{2}+|q|K}{n}-\frac{1}{n}\log E_{\epsilon}(\varphi).

Now
|E_{\epsilon}(\varphi)|\leq C_{2}(\epsilon)
implies

\displaystyle f_{n}(q)\leq\mbox{\rm Pr}_{\sigma}(g+q\Psi)+\frac{|q|K}{n}+\frac{C(\epsilon)}{n}

for some global constant
C(\epsilon)>0
depending only on
\epsilon
. This proves the lemma.
